[trialAccount t18440205-746]
746. THOMAS SARGEANT was indicted for stealing 1 sovereign, 1 half-sovereign, 3 crowns, 6 half-crowns, 14 shillings, 2 sixpences, 30 four penny pieces, 30 pence, and 16 halfpence, the monies of Francis Tucker and others, his masters.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN MURRELL . I am porter to Francis Tucker and two others, of South Moulton-street. In consequence of observations I made of the shop cash, during Aug. and Sept., I made a communication to my employers—I afterwards observed the entries made in the cash-book—I took particular observation of the dripping and other stuff that was purchased during the 9th of Sept., 1843—on that day John Tarrant, who is usually in the shop, went to Gravesend—I remained there besides the prisoner—we were the only persons in the shop—at two o'clock the prisoner went to dinner—I looked at the dripping and the change—the dripping is kept in a large tab in the cellar, and the change on a board about three feet by two and a half—the tub would contain about three hundredweight of dripping or a little more—I took the trowel which is usually kept in the cellar and smoothed the surface of the dripping in the tub, and I made a cross on it all the way across the tub—I then examined the changes on the board—there was about two stone of it, just sufficient to cover the board—I returned to the shop and looked into the drawer which is used for the reception of dripping in the shop, before it is taken down to the tub in the cellar—I found the drawer quite empty—I have the shop cash-book here—it was the prisoner's duty to purchase dripping and articles of that description in the shop, and enter them in the book—while he was out that day I examined this cash-book—I found the last entry was two stone of changes, by which it appears that they had been delivered before he went to dinner, and there was about two stone of changes on the board or dresser—on the prisoner's return about three, I left the shop—I returned a little after four—I
then looked at the shop cash-book and found an entry of thirty-seven pounds of dripping, 9 s. 3 d., and two stone four pounds of changes 5 s. 5 d., making together 14 s. 8 d.—I went in the cellar and examined the tub, and found there had been no dripping whatever added to it—I looked in the dripping drawer, and none had been put in there—I looked at the dresser, where the changes were kept, and none had been added there—the cross which I had made on the dripping in the tub was quite visible, and if thirty-seven pounds had been added to it that would not have been the case—on the 25th of Sept. Tarrant returned from Gravesend and I made a communication to him on this subject—here is the shop cash-book—it is balanced on the 8th of Sept., and the balance carried forward in Tarrant's writing—he went to Gravesend about twelve o'clock, and on that morning this book came into the prisoner's possession—he had to examine it and see that the cash corresponded with the entries—he balanced it on the evening of the 9th, and the balance is 4 1/2 d. over in the till, which will often occur—the balance brought forward is 78 l.; 19 s. 8 d.—it was the prisoner's duty to pay for changes and dripping out of the shop-till.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. I warrant, as soon as you made this discovery on thr 9th, you went to your master? A. I did not say a word about it till my brother shopman came back—I do not know how much money, per month, would pass through the prisoner's hands—it might be 7000 l.; or 8000 l.;—there was only one receptacle for dripping in use at that time, besides the drawer—we have several tubs which are used at different times.
Q. Would not the prisoner frequently purchase in the morning and not enter them till the evening? A. It is not a usual thing—I have frequently seen him purchase dripping—I do not swear that on every occasion he has entered it—I do not know that he has not done so—I have not made any mistakes—I know Mr. Clarkson—I believe he keeps a little shop in Ebury-row—I do not remember his borrowing three papers of Palmer's wicks from our house—he frequently borrows them—I do not remember his borrowing three pupen of Palmer's wicks—I did not go to Mrs. Clarkson and tell her that I had forgotten to enter three pounds of metallic wicks, and ask her what I should do, nor did I say, "You must not let our people know it, for if you do, Sargeant will find it out and kick up the d—I's own row about it"—I will swear there was not an omission of entering three pounds of Palmer's candles—the prisoner has never found fault with my conduct—there might be a few little words perhaps—I do not remember omitting to enter some goods purchased by Mrs. Clarkson, on the 24th of Dec.—I will not swear I did not omit it—I do not remember an entry that should have been made on the 27th of Jan. that was not inserted till Feb.—I swear that was not the case—I never make entries in the book—I make them on a slip of paper—I have on every occasion made that entry at the time the sale took place—I never failed to do so—I am porter to the prosecutors—it might be the case sometimes, that when goods were delivered at our house, instead of paying for them at the time, a ticket was given to the person who left them, and they would come some days after and receive the money; but that would be entered on the day on which the goods were bought, not on the day on which the cash was paid—I am certain of that, because there is always a ticket left in the till, and that is brought in as the money paid out—it stands as cash in the book—when the cash is paid the ticket is then destroyed—I know that to be invariably the case—I have seen it done—I do not keep the books.
JURY. Q. Do you take the cash out for that ticket? A. Yes, and it is put in a piece of paper with the ticket—I think there is one family that sell their own dripping and wax ends instead of allowing it to their servant
the dripping and wax ends come from Lord Liverpool's house to our house for sale—I do not know what his lordship does—other noblemen and gentlemen send their dripping and wax ends—I do not know whether it is the practice of our house to allow the house-steward or the principal servant 1 d. a pound on every article brought—there is a perquisite allowed—I do not know whether that 1 d. a pound would be put down as a separate item—the prisoner's department was in the counting-house—he did not usually attend the shop, only when other people were absent, but he might come into the shop—I do not know who has got the prisoner's place, or whether it is settled—my wages were raised about five weeks ago, or a little more—it was since this affair.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you look at all the tubs in which dripping was usually put? A. Yes, and I examined them all on my return—these tickets are given by the person in charge of the shop at the time, to those who bring the goods instead of cash, as perhaps we might not have change at the time—there was no deficiency of cash on the 9th of Sept.—if any of these tickets had been given after twelve o'clock that day, the money would appear among the cash—one ticket is given to the person, and one is put into the till, and in the evening, when we have sufficient change, the money is rolled up in the paper for the persons when they come for it—I am a yearly servant, and am paid quarterly—my wages have been raised from the 1st of Jan.—they were 16 l.; a-year, and are now 18 l.;
JURY. Q. Did you when you returned examine all the tubs? A. Yes—there was only one that had dripping in—I looked into the others, and none of them had any.
JOHN TABRANT . I am shopman to Messrs. Tucker and Co. I went to Gravesend on the 9th of Sept., about twelve o'clock—on the evening of the 8th I balanced the cash-book—I returned on the 25th, and on that evening Murrell made a communication to me, and on the 26th I communicated with one of the partners—the money in the till on the evening of the 8th corresponded with the cash-book, when I balanced it.
Cross-examined. Q. Do not some of the nobility and gentry keep an account at your house for dripping and wax ends? A. Yes—I do not know that the servants are allowed 1 d. a pound on dripping or wax ends brought to our house—if we buy the wax ends of servants, of course we give them the money—there is something allowed to the servant for himself—it is entered in the book as for goods bought—I have never seen the prisoner make entries in the evening, of goods that he had purchased in the morning—I will swear that he has never done so—I go to dinner at one o'clock, and to tea at five—I sometimes go to the Bank, and sometimes to collect money—I am perhaps absent two hours—the prisoner has found fault with me for what he said Was negligence—he has reprimanded me for neglect in making entries in the book—I am Mrs. Tucker's nephew—I remember some time ago omitting to enter 721bs. of candles sold to Messrs. Ferguson and Co., of Oxford-street, and some sold to Watkins'—I do not remember omitting to enter goods sold to Hughes, in May Fair.
Q. Do you remember receiving goods from Mr. Clarkson that you did not enter till some days after, when the mistake was discovered? A. I do not know whether I omitted it, the porter might have done so as well as myself—when Clarkson brought his invoice, he said there was something omitted, and I entered it then, and he paid the money to me—it does sometimes happen that parties deliver dripping and wax ends, and receive a ticket for them and do not get the money for several days—in that case the ticket is put by, and entered in the book on the day the money is paid sometimes, and sometimes not.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. You said something about servants, what is that? A. The wax ends are considered the perquisite of the butler or footman, and when they bring them we give them less than we do to a collector, but whatever we receive from a collector, or from a servant, we make an entry in the book of it, so that the book shows everything—there is no separate sum paid to a servant, except for the amount we buy—when the omission which has been asked about occurred, I had not received any money which I had not accounted for, but I had left out an item in the customer's bill, and my omiting it caused the prisoner's displeasure.
THOMAS MOSDELL SMITH . I am a partner in the house—Tarrant and Murrell were in our employ—I recollect another servant going to Gravesend, and on that occasion a communication was made to me indirectly by John Tarrant.
Cross-examined. Q. Has it not very often happened that checks have been cashed for your customers, or for neighbours? A. No, it never happened to my knowledge—my department is the superintendence of the cash account and correspondence, not the manufacturing department—our house keeps no account with any nobleman or gentleman for dripping or wax ends, to my knowledge.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What do you mean by saying there is no account with any nobleman or gentleman? A. I know there is one family where the dripping which is supplied by them goes to their credit—in general where there is dripping it goes to the account of the cook.
FREDERICK SHAW ( police-sergeant A 29.) I took the prisoner on the 11th of Jan.—I produce a paper which was found by Mr. Smith in the counting-house—it was sealed up—I directed Mr. Smith to break it open.
MR. SMITH re-examined. I found this paper at the bottom of the cash drawer in the iron safe in the counting-house, the name of T. Sergeant is on it—I broke it open—I delivered it to Mr. Blackmore in the same state as it was.
ROBERT BLACKMORE . I am attorney for this prosecution. I received this paper and its contents from Mr. Smith, and delivered it to the officer—it contains a 5 l.; note and a sovereign.
ISAAC BLOMFIELD . Iam servant to Mr. Edridge, of Oxford-street. On the 7th of Nov. last I went to the prosecutor's shop, in South Moulton-street—I paid the prisoner, who appeared to be a clerk there, 9 l.; 3 s. 10 d.—part of it was a 5 l.; note—this is the note I paid him that day—I know it by the writing on it.
Cross-examined. Q. What is the writing? A. The name of Mr. Freshfield, 24, Rutland-street, Hampstead-road—when the prosecution was going on they came to my employer, who is a master tallow-chandler, in Oxford-street—(paper read)—"This 6 l.; is money over in the shop cash, supposed to belong to T. Serjeant, his own money not being right, and no other account can be given of it; it will be left in the cash drawer, to see if anything else comes to light on the subject. Nov. 4, 1843."
MR. SMITH re-examined. Q. Supposing there was money over or under in the shop cash-book, what was the prisoner's duty? A. To tell me of it—here is the shop cash-book for the 4th of Nov.—here appears to have been 3 1/2 d. over that day—the prisoner never gave me any account of there being 6 l.; over in the shop cash.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440205-747]
747. THOMAS SARGEANT was again indicted for stealing 2 sovereigns,3 half-sovereigns, 6 crowns, 12 half-crowns, 29 shillings, 58 sixpences, 85 fourpenny-pieces, 3 pence, and 6 halfpence, the monies of Francis Tucker and other, his masters.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS MOSDELL SMITH . I am one of the firm of Francis Tucker and others. On Wednesday, the 10th of Jan., in consequence of suspicion, I saw all the sperm and wax ends and scrapings that I had in stock, weighed, about two o'clock—Murrell and Tarrant were in attendance on me in so doing—the weight of the whole was 471lbs. 8ozs., and there appeared by the shop cash-book 13ozs. of scrapings bought that afternoon, which would make the whole weight 472lbs. 5ozs.—I used all the precaution in my power to ascertain the exact quantity—next day, the 11th, about ten ruinates to five o'clock, I gave directions to Shaw, the policeman, and I put three sovereigns and 2 l.; worth of shillings, all marked, into the till—there is a well in the till under where the money is kept loose, in which there is a bag with other gold in it—I do not see any entry made in the book by the prisoner on the 10th of Jan.—this entry of 13ozs. is by Tarrant—on the 11th of Jan., about half-past five, I found the prisoner had entered in the shop cash-book as having paid 13lbs. 8ozs. 8 drachms of wax ends 1 l.;, 4lbs. of sperm 5 s. 3 d., and dripping 3 s., making in the whole 1 l.; 8 s. 3 d.—I directly called in the policeman, and took him into the counting-house—I called in the prisoner, and said, "Sargeant, here is an entry I don't exactly understand, when did you buy these things?"—he hesitated, and at length said, "In the morning at eleven o'clock"—I said, "That answer does not satisfy me, I have strong doubts as to your integrity, will you show me the contents of your pockets?"—he threw a leathern purse on the desk—I said, "You have more money about you than this"—he then placed his hands in his pocket, and put on the desk a quantity of silver, I believe all in shillings—I found amongst them eight of the shillings which I had marked on the Wednesday and put them into the till on the Thursday at ten minutes past five during his absence, and this conversation took place about a quarter before six o'clock—I gave him in charge—I said to him, "To save me the trouble of weighing the stock again, the weight of which I know perfectly well, will you tell me where the things are that you say you bought?"—he replied, "You seem to know all about it; I don't say"—the policeman said, "You need answer no questions, but do you choose to account for having the marked money upon you?"—he replied, "I don't say"—I omitted to state that when I discovered the marked silver amongst his money I could not discover the marked sovereign—I had put three marked sovereigns into the till, and only two of them remained—one of them was gone—I then sent for the bag which was placed in the well of the till, and in that bag I found the marked sovereign—the prisoner was then taken to the station, and I proceeded to weigh the wax ends and other things, and allowing for the 13ozs. being added to it, the real increase was 3ozs., allowing for the turn of the scale—it was quite impossible that I that morning at ten minutes to ten o'clock, and the prisoner was engaged in my presence from about ten minutes to eleven till twenty-five minutes to twelve, and in that time he made no purchase of wax ends, or scrapings, or dripping—he never left the counting-house—I was engaged in an interview with a gentleman in a small place that adjoins the counting-house—while the prisoner was not engaged with me he was with Mr. Harrison in the counting-house—I can undertake to say that from ten minutes to eleven till twenty-five minutes to twelve o'clock he-was not out of the counting-house.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. What did you weigh? A. The whole of the wax candle ends, and scrapings, and dripping, on the premises—I weighed the drawers in which they were placed, but I gave the net weight—the tare was marked on the drawers, and the deduction was made—I looked myself into every drawer, to see that there was none but what were weighed—I will not swear as to the number of receptacles the people had, but I will swear there were no more than what were weighed—a regular search was made all over the place, to see that there was no more—I did not weigh anything down stairs—the drawers are the receptacles in the shop, and when they are full they are sent away—there is a cupboard in the shop—I swear that we weighed the whole contents of that—I kept the numeration of the figures—I weighed first at two o'clock, on Wednesday; again, at two o'clock, on Thursday; and again, in the evening after the prisoner was in custody—Murrell and Tarrant were with me every time I weighed it—if the prisoner had told me the drawer in which what he said was bought, was placed, I would have weighed that drawer, but he refused to tell me where the things were—he had not frequent occasions to go to the till where the money was kept—if the porter or shopman were on the premises it was their duty to make those purchases—the prisoner's duty was in the counting-house, not the shop, but, in the absence of the others, it was his duty—we had no scarcity of silver at that time—there was a scarcity, but we had 60 l.; or 80 l.; worth—I never knew the prisoner to lend to the till, or to borrow of the till—there was plenty in the till.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. When you went to the till, after you gave notice to the policeman, did you find the silver you had put there? A. Yes, and on the prisoner I found eight of the marked shillings—there had been no want of silver—I had put entire confidence in the prisoner.
JOHN MURRELL . On the Wednesday, at two o'clock, I attended my master, to weigh all the wax ends and scrapings that there were—they were all brought to the scale on that occasion—Tarrant attended and assisted—I weighed again at two o'clock on Thursday, when all that could be found was again produced—after the prisoner was taken we again weighed them—the weight on all occasions was the same, with the difference of three ounces at 'last—I went to the premises on Thursday morning, the 11th of Jan., at seven o'clock—the prisoner did not sleep on the premises—he came a little afterseven that morning—he was in my sight till eight o'clock, when he went to breakfast—he returned at nine o'clock, and from then till a quarter to twelve he was in my sight—during that time he did not make a purchase of anything, if he had I must have seen it—I was watching for that purpose
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose you have various duties to perform? A. I have—I had not occasion to go out from seven to twelve o'clock—I breakfast on the premises—I went to breakfast at the time the prisoner did—I returned in half an hour, and I was there half an hour before he was back—I went out at a quarter to twelve, and returned about half-past twelve—I left the shop after two o'clock in the afternoon—I do not know how many boxes were weighed, but I called the weights out correctly—we weighed the wax, the sperm, the dripping, and every thing we had on the premises—I cannot recollect the number of receptacles there were for the wax ends and dripping.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. There are certain receptacles for wax ends, and scrapings, and sperm? A. Yes, in the shop, and there are others for dripping and changes below—we went round, and weighed all that was on the premises—we weighed the dripping also—there were not twelve pounds addition to that—I had marked the dripping-tub with a cross, and that cross was
there the same—it had been arranged that I should not be absent when Tarrant was, nor he be absent when I was—we do not know who the collectors are, but if we take any wax ends or scrapings in we know where we put them.
JOHN TARRANT . I was on the premises on the Thursday, from half-past nine till one o'clock, when I went up to dinner—the prisoner could not have purchased wax ends, or dripping, or sperm, that morning, without my seeing him—I had been desired to watch—he purchased no articles that morning—I returned from dinner at half-past one, and then I remained there till five—I might have been absent for two minutes, but he could not have bought any thing in that time—I went out once when Murrell was not there—the prisoner was in the counting-house when I went out, and he was there when I came back—I balanced the shop cash-book on that Thursday, and it balanced, supposing these goods had been paid for—here is a deduction of 1 l.; 8 s. 3 d., from the shop cash that day by the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you not said, on a former occasion, that you were out from time to time? A. I was not out more than two minutes—I had occasion to go down stairs, but I could hear any one come into the shop, and it is impossible that in that time he could have bought that quantity—I went up stairs once with a letter, but Murrell was in the shop then—I never knew the prisoner borrow money from the till, or lend money to it—I have borrowed money of him when I have not had sufficient change.
JOHN MARKS . I am accountant in the prosecutor's establishment. On the 11th of Jan. I got there about five minutes to ten o'clock—the prisoner was engaged with me the whole morning, except when receiving a small sum of money from Mr. Hall—he made no purchase over the counter—if he had, I think I must have observed it.
FREDERICK SHAW ( police-sergeant A 29.) On the 11th of Jan. I went to South Moulton-street, by directions from Mr. Smith—the shopman and porter went away, and during their absence I watched the shop—no person took any thing to sell that day—I saw the prisoner go to the till while no person was in the shop—I crossed the road, and looked through the window—he took something from a bag in the till, and appeared to be counting it—I thought it was money—he had it in his left hand, and was running it over—after that he went into the counting-house—about a quarter of an hour after that Mr. Smith took in the shop cash-book, and he said to the prisoner, "Sergeant, here is an entry I do not understand"—he asked him when these goods had been purchased—the prisoner said, "This morning, at eleven o'clock"—Mr. Smith said, "Why was not the entry made at the time?"—he said he had omitted it—Mr. Smith said, "I don't feel satisfied, I should like to see what you have in your pocket"—he gave him his purse, in which was a check and some money—he then put his hand into his pocket, and pulled out some silver, and amongst it were these eight marked shillings, which I now produce—Mr. Smith asked in what drawer he had placed what he bought—he said he seemed to know all about it, and he did not say.
(William Smart, a coal-merchant; Matthew Bramer, a coal-merchant; Edward Smith, a cheesemonger; Thomas Knight, a butcher; William Howell, a plumber; Charles Meredith, a painter; James Newman, a currier; Thomas Wheeler; David Probert, a carpenter; Ambrose Cook, a tailor; David Bowyer; and John Ingliss, a saddler; gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 36.— Transported for Seven Years.

749. WILLIAM SLOUGH was indicted for stealing 12 muffineers, value 5 l.; 8 s.; 2 cruet-frames, 7 l.; 16 cruets, 2 l.; 3 dishes, 13 l.; 1 liquear-frame, 1 l.; 3 bottles, 3 l.; 1 tureen, 13 l.; 1 snuffer-tray mounting, 2 s. 2 candlesticks. 3 l.; 1 cream-jug, 1 l.; 16 s.; 1 muffineer-top, 2 s.; and 1 packing-case, 5 s.; the goods of Joseph Alexander and another, his masters.—2nd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of Samuel Roberts and others.—3rd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of Frederick Hunt.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM SISSONS . I am of the firm of Roberts and Co.—we have a house at Sheffield, and another in the Adelphi. On the night of the 19th of Jan. I filled a chest at Sheffield, and directed it to the Adelphi—it contained the articles stated, worth altogether 53 l.; 15 s.—it was nailed up, and a printed direction put on it, "S. Roberts, Smith, and Co., Robert-street, Adelphi, London"—the date, "19th of Jan.," and "Carriage paid," put on it—it was delivered to the usual porter, to be forwarded by railway—I have not seen it since.
JOHN TUCKER . I am clerk to Joseph Alexander. It is part of my duty to attend the Birmingham Railway, at Camden-town station, and to receive parcels arriving in London—I have the way-bill of the 22nd of Jan.—there it a box directed to Roberts, Smith, and Co.—I saw it, and the direction on it—it was handed to the van by the prisoner, and it was his duty to deliver it at the direction—there was a boy with him—I did not see the prisoner again till about six o'clock in the evening—he said he had lost the box, or had it stolen in the neighbourhood of Charing-cross—I asked what time—he said be saw it in the van about one, and he missed it between that and three—I said I thought it looked very suspicious, losing it at that hour in the day.
Cross-examined by MR. COBBETT. Q. You have known him some time? A. Yes—he has been in Mr. Alexander's employment many years.
JOSEPH ALEXANDER . I keep the Castle and Falcon, and am agent to the carriers from Sheffield—the prisoner was in my employ to carry parcels—on the evening of the 22nd Jan.; between six and seven o'clock, he came and said he had lost a box for Messrs. Roberts and Co., he did not know how it had gone, that he saw it at Northumberland-house, at one, where he had to deliver goods—that he had to go down to Westminster, and got to the Adelpbi about three—he then looked and missed the box—I asked if he had met any one during the day—he denied that he had—I then called the boy Simpkins, and questioned him—I then called the prisoner in, and before the prisoner the boy said they met a man named Holmes, in the Hampstead-road, and they went in the public-house and had something to drink, Simpkins standing in the doorway watching the van—that they went to deliver goods—when the bof said they met Holmes, the prisoner said his name was Jesse—Cooper wai sitting in my tap about half-past seven on the 22nd of Jan.—he gave me thif paper while the prisoner was in my office—I do not know the prisoner's handwriting.
FREDERICK RUSSELL ( City police-constable, No. 203.) I was sent for, and took the prisoner—I found this paper on him, on which, in pencil, were these words, "Wni. Holmes, 11, Wellington-street, Camden-town, clear the lot away as soon as possible, there is a move; I look up."
ROBERT EDWARD SIMPKINS . I accompanied the prisoner on the 22nd of Jan. with the parcels from the Birmingham Railway to go about town with them—there was a box for Roberts, Smith, and Co.—we got to Northumberland-house, and delivered four boxes—we then went to Broadway, Westminster, where we had six bags of nails to deliver—on my return I did not notice whether the box was in the van—when we got into the Strand the prisoner said, "Oh, my God, Teddy, the box is gone"—we went to the Hampstead-road before that, and met Holmes the cabman —the prisoner went into the public-house with Holmes, and I was on the van waiting on the other side the road—he was there twenty minutes—the prisoner then came to the van and drove off—I knew Holmes by sight—I did not know where he lived—the prisoner told me his name was Holmes—when we got to the Castle and Falcon my master sent for me and talked to me about the matter—I told him we went to the public-house and met Holmes—the prisoner said we had met a man—I was absent from the cart at Westminster while I was delivering the parcel and getting my book signed for it—that would afford a man the opportunity of taking the box—when we were in the Hampstead-road that morning and met Holmes, I asked the prisoner if that was not the man we met in the Minories some time before—he said, "No, that man is dead and buried"—the box could not have tumbled off.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it not on the roof of the cart? A. No—in the middle of the van, on the two rails—there was a box or two underneath it—it was between nine and ten o'clock when we met Holmes—we got to the Broadway between two and three o'clock—I left the cart long enough for any person to take the goods away—when I was in the Broadway the prisoner turned the cart to a public-house, and I could not see it.
MR. CLARKBON. Q. When you went to take the nails, who did you leave in the cart? A. The prisoner.
JAMES BRISTOW COOPER , cook, Crouch-end, Paddington. On the 22nd Jan. I was out of employ—in the evening the prisoner came into the tap-room of the Castle and Falcon and asked for pen, ink, and paper—a female gave him pen and ink, but said she had no paper—I offered to give him a piece of paper—I tore it off the back of a letter and gave it him—he wrote upon it, folded it up, wafered it, and gave it to me, and said, "Cooper, you take that directly for me, I will give you 1 s., for your trouble in the morning"—before I went I heard of this loss—in consequence of that I gave this paper to Mr. Alexander—I did not take notice of the prisoner's writing—I saw him fold it up and direct it—this is it—it is written on the back of a letter addressed to Mr. Cooper, Castle and Falcon.
FREDERICK RUSSELL re-examined. I took the prisoner, and read this paper—I went to Wellington-street the same night—I there took Holmes, but the Magistrate discharged him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you say anything as to whether it would be better for him to tell you? A. No—I did not say "It is a pity you have not told your master all about it"—we have got the box.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. What was it he said to you? A. In going through the hospital, he asked me if I thought he should be transported—I said, "I know nothing at all about it, but don't you think you had better tell your master the truth?"—he said he met Holmes and a man named Jack in Hampstead-road; as he was coming down the road they stopped him, and told him they should like to have one of the boxes in the van; he said he did not know what to say to that; they told him, if he would let them have one, there would be a good booty of it coming to him, and when he came to Westminster the box went away, but he could not tell whether it was Holmes or
this man Jack —I asked who he was—he said a man who was to be seen in all parts of London as porter, he did not know any more of him—I asked where they were to bring his share to—he said to the Castle and Falcon.
CHARTES JAMES MOATE . I am clerk to Roberts, Smith, and Co. This parcel did not arrive at our premises.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Seven Years.

750. WILLIAM ALDERSON was indicted for stealing 71bs. weight of bacon, value 3 s. 9 d., the goods of Daniel Arthur Sykes.
DANIEL ARTHUR SYKES . I live in New-end, Hampstead-lane. On the 7th of Feb. I saw the prisoner in my shop—he took a piece of bacon off the counter, and threw it to a person at the door—I came into the shop—he asked for a pennyworth of tobacco—I asked what he had done with the bacon—he denied taking it.

[trialAccount t18440205-751]
751. SAMUEL MULLETT was indicted for stealing 6 jugs, value 3 s.; 14 cups, 5 s.; 17 saucers, 3 s.; 6 cruets and tops, 10 s.; 4 chimney ornaments, 15 s.; 5 basins, 1 s.; 2 milk-pots, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 tea-pot, 2 s.; 6 plates, 3 s.; 1 basket 1 s.; and 2 twopenny pieces; the property of Samuel Smith.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL SMITH . I am an omnibus proprietor, and lodge at the King's Arms, Fulham; I formerly kept it. I first knew the prisoner as a waterman—I know the members of the Association at Fulham, and recommended him to them as a conductor—on the 10th of Nov. he was at my house—Mr. Webb came down from the meeting—a quarrel took place between the prisoner and Webb—he used very bad language, and I said it did not become him, he was getting a living under us—my wife was in the bar; she interfered in the quarrel; I told her it was no business whatever of hers, gave her a brush with my band, and said, "Go along"—a summons was taken out against roe on Monday, and Iappear—on the Saturday I received information; on Sunday morning I went to my drawers, and found they had been stripped of their contents—I made my wife put on her things, and leave the house—she went across to the prisoner's father's—I went to my drawers, and found the whole of their contents gone—in the room where my wife was I found a large portmanteau belonging to the prisoner, nailed and corded, in readiness to be removed—I did not allow them to be moved away—my wife never returned home—on the Tuesday I went with the constable to Mr. Gray's, in Drummond-street, at five in the morning, and in the back room, first floor, we found the prisoner and my wife in bed together—I gave them into custody, and they were taken to the station in Albany-street—they were discharged at the station—I afterwards followed them to Ireland, and gave them into custody—I went to where they lived in Ireland, and found six jugs, fourteen cups, seventeen saucers, six cruets and tops, four chimney ornaments, and other things, in a basket, and two twopenny-pieces in the prisoner's box—they are all my property, and the prisoner had seen them used at my house.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. What were you originally? A. I was driving at Clapham before I took the house at Fulham—I was an omnibus conductor for a very short time—when the quarrel took place between the prisoner and Webb, I did not knock my wife down—she did not stagger with the blow—I will swear that I did not strike her a violent blow—the only part that I struck her was my nail was rather long, and cut the corner of her eye—I do not know whether it bled—we had a slight quarrel once, and I threw
my hat at her—I never remember kicking her—I never did to my knowledge—I have never had my lip cut by a woman since I have been married—the-only time I was away from my wife was three or four days, while I had a Wandsworth omnibus—I was lodging at Wandsworth, not with a female—I have never been intimate with another female since I have been married—I have not been in the habit of gambling—the only gambling I ever transacted was in the house which I used to keep, where it was usual for us to play at 3 d. a corner, and sometimes 6 d. or 1 s.—I know a man named Hartly—I swear that the extent of my gambling has been 1 s. 6 d.—I never won in one nigbt at my house upwards of 30 l.; nor any such sum—Hartly has a situation in a banking-house—there was no raffle in my house for a horse—there was at Mr. Hartly's house—I met Mr. Briant that night at Mr. Hardly's house—I and Briant did not divide between us what we had won that night—I cannot say whether I and Briant won that night—I made some little bets, but I cannot say whether I was a winner or loser—I did not pretend that night to lose 6 l.; to Hartly—I do not think I lost 1 l.;—I have not boasted in my own parlour how I and Briant had nailed Hartly—the card-playing went on in the back-parlour—we never went into a private room up stairs—it was a regular thing every night in the back parlour—it might go on up stain in private—my wife has not remonstrated with me, and told me I should ruin her, and that I was killing her by inches, in keeping her up—she has remonstrated with me when we have been later than usual of a night—I do not remember when I was gaming in the middle of the day my wife wanting to put the table-cloth on for dinner, and my laying hold of the table-cloth and kicking her—Warboys came to me at Wandsworth, to persuade me to come home to my wife—I am sure he has only done that once—I have not been away from her more than once, only the last time I was at Margate for ill health, when the prisoner was sleeping in my bed—I wished my wife to go down—she would not—Mr. Warboys offered to mind the house for my wife, if she would go—I first saw this portmanteau on Sunday morning—this basket was found at Dublin, but I saw a basket on the Tuesday, where these things were kept—the prisoner was in the house when I found this crockery—I claimed it then—my wife said, "There is nothing else belonging to you"—I identified it, and pulled out two china greyhounds, which I identified—the policeman and Puddeford were there, they went to Ireland with me—I took them before a Magistrate in Ireland—upon my oath I then charged them with stealing this crockery—they were given to the officer to bring to England—the Irish Magistrate said he should have committed them if it had been his case—my wife purchased the crockery for the house—she did not bring me any property—after the death of her mother she brought me 400 l.;—I gave my wife 80 l.; or 90 l.; to purchase things for the house—she purchased the things for my house—she superintended.
MILKS HURDNOT . I was formerly pot-boy to the prosecutor. On the Thursday before Mrs. Smith left I saw the prisoner, with a hamper in his hand, in the parlour, by the side of the gateway, at the back of the premises—he was packing the hamper with some cups and saucers—I do not know where they came from—I should not know them again—they were different to these—this hand-basket belonged to Mrs. Smith—I had seen both the baskets about the place—I had seen these baskets used by Smith—after the prisoner had packed up the things he told me to come in ten minutes, and when I went in ten minutes it was already corded—he lifted it on my back, and told me to take it to his father's back premises, and told me it was paint for his father—my master had gone up with the bust to London, and my mistress was in the parlour.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440205-752]
752. SAMUEL MULLETT was again indicted for stealing 1 penknife, value 1 s. 6 d.; 2 bags, 6 d.; 1 purse, 1 s. 6 d.; 25 sovereigns, 19 half-crowns, 37 shillings, and 20 sixpences; the property of Samuel Smith.
HANNAH SMITH . I am the prosecutor's wife, and was found in Drummond-street with the prisoner—before I left my husband I had some silver, partly new silver, which was kept in my bed-room—part of it accompanied us to Ireland—I fetched the money out of my bed-room about five months before I left home—I said I had no money—the prisoner said, "Yes, you have"—I said, "I have some up stairs, but I cannot let you have it"—he said, "Fetch it down"—I fetched it, and placed it in the bar drawer, telling him at the same time not to touch it for God's sake, for fear Smith should find it out—(I surrendered my person to the prisoner about two years before)—he said, "D—d nonsense"—he took the money way—I do not know what he did with it—to the best of my recollection there was 10 l.; or 11 l.;—I am not certain—he had it in his possession in Ireland in the bag—the bag bore some initials—I cannot say what they were—I went to Drummond-street on the Sunday—my husband turned me out from home—the prisoner had the money in his possession in Drum mond-street—my husband came and found us together—the money was then found in the prisoner's box—I saw it produced—the prisoner claimed it—I am sure that was the money I brought down stairs—there were twenty-nine sovereigns in a white purse—the purse was the prisoner's, but the money was mine—he claimed it—he put them in some time before, because I was in the habit of giving him money—he promised if I got a certain quantity of money he would take me to Ireland—he took the silver out of the drawer, and I gave him the gold at home at our house.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. Did your husband turn you out of the house? A. Yes; he told me to go out—he did once beat me—we never had any quarrels until I became acquainted with the prisoner—my husband has frequently beaten me—he never kicked me—I have been living with my sister in the Borough—my husband has not visited me there—he has been to the house—I have seen him, but he has not seen me—he has seen the child—Icame here on my own account—I met Mr. Warboys here, a friend of my husband's—I was not here yesterday, or the day before—I was here the beginning of last week—I came in case I should be wanted—no one told me I might be wanted—I volunteered my evidence here—I said when I was in Dublin that all the property was my own, and the prisoner knew nothing about it, that I was turned out, and sought protection from him, and he gave me that protection, that I sold him the knife found in his pocket for a half-penny, and he had committed no robbery, that it was me who got him into trouble, and I deemed it my duty to try and get him out, but I did it by the prisoner's persuasion—I fetched the silver down because he wished me—my husband allowed me to take it—I am not aware that I stated in Dublin that nothing occurred between me and the prisoner till my husband turned me out—upon my oath I did not say so—I know Mr. Jennings, the attorney—I told him that every shilling of the money I had given to the prisoner, but I said it by the prisoner's persuasion—my sister has been supporting me—she keeps the Black Bull in the Borough—I have not told any one what I had to say to-day—this purse I bought with my own money and gave to the prisoner.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did the prisoner know whose knife it was? A. He knew it was mine—Mr. Jennings came to me at Hammersmith, and I told him I had been very indifferently used by my husband at different times, and I was then going as evidence for the prisoner—I was not examined as a witness—the prisoner told me to be sure and stick to what I said, and I promised
I would—the money I gave him was my husband's—I had none of my own—I had had connexion with the prisoner before I gate him toe money.
WILLIAM THORNTON . I was horse-keeper to the prosecutor in Aug. and Sept.—I was at Fulham on a Wednesday when my matter was absent—I saw the prisoner at the house just before twelve o'clock—Mrs. Smith was up—no one else was in the house—I was in the tap-room, and Mrs. Smith told me it was time to go to bed—I went up stairs to go to bed in my room-after I went up stairs the prisoner followed Mrs. Smith up stairs to her bedroom—I saw Mrs. Smith draw the curtains and put out the light.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you never said one word about this to any one till two days after Mrs. Smith left? A. No.
JAMES GRAY , tobacconist, Drunmond-street, Euston-square. On Sunday, the 12th of Nov., the prisoner came to my bouse with Mrs. Smith—they asked for apartments for a few nights—I let them the first floor—they lived there as man and wife—they were found in bed at my house, by the officer snd Mr. Smith, at five o'clock in the morning—tbe prisoner brought three packages, which were directed to some person in Dublin—they returned to my house the same day as they were taken in custody, and left in half or three quarters of an hour—there was a hamper there, which was taken away to Liverpool, to be forwarded to Dublin.
Cross-examined. Q. Mrs. Smith was very fond of the prisoner? A. Yes.
EDWARD BROWN . I am a cabman, and stand in the rank in Drummond-street—on Sunday evening, the 12th of Nov., the prisoner came and asked if I could drive to Fulham—I said, "Yes"—he said he wanted some luggage brought back—I drove him to Fulham—he went to a house—he put a hamper and portmanteau, a canvas bag, a gun-case, and a deal box in the cab —he told me to drive on about twenty yards from the house, and he would come to me—he came, and told me to make the best of my way home to where I came from, which was Mr. Gray's house in Drummond-street—next day I went again with him to Fulham, and set him down about 200 yards before he got to his father's house—he told me to pull down to the door and bring the baggage away—I received from him a large chest and a hat-box—he ordered me to drive back, and stop at the same place that I had come. from—I said, "Yes," and he told me to take the left-hand road—I left him at Gray's.
SAMUEL SMITH . I remember my wife leaving home by my direction—I had before that deposited with her some silver; I cannot say what amount—I should think from the appearance of the bag, the last time I saw it, there must have been 7 l.; or 8 l.;—this is the bag—this shilling I can speak to—I was not aware that at any time before this my wife had parted with any of this money to the prisoner—I traced them to Drummond-street, and the prisoner took this bag out of his box—there was another little bag in this bag—it was my habit to trust my wife with the care of the money—when I got to Drummond-street I saw this bag, and said I had no doubt of its being mine, but I was in such an agitated state that I hardly remember what did transpire—I do not know what has become of the little bag—it was given up to the prisoner, he claiming it—my initials were on it—it was the bag that I had had in my house, and had delivered to my wife to take care of my money in—I have been missing money for a length of time, and complaining of it—I expected to find 200 l.; in the house, which I did not—the prisoner
said the money was his.
Cross-examined. Q. How do you know this shilling? A. From the different appearance and feel of it—I did not take it away in my pocket—it was
handed to me to identify, and I produced it from my pocket—the Magistrate told me to give it up to the officer—I might have given my wife a slap on the face several times, but not to have done her injury—the prisoner has been separated from his wife.
SAMUEL PUDDIFORD ( police-constable T 127.) I went to Dublin with Mr. Smith—I had a warrant against the prisoner for deserting his wife and children—I did not know his wife—I found this bag of silver money in the bottom of the prisoner's box in Ireland—he told me it was his box.
Cross-examined. Q. Who obtained the parish warrant? A. It was given by the Magistrate—I do not know who applied for it—Mr. Inspector Smith gave itine.
SAMUEL SMITH re-examined. This purse I found in Drummond-street, under the prisoner's pillow—he snatched at it, and said, "They are mine"—I said, "No, that one belongs to me, I know; I have carried money in it some time "—he threw it down—there were 14 s. or 15 s. in it—I took it and kept it—there was some gold in another under his pillow—from the appearance there must have been 30 l.; in it—the prisoner claimed it, and it was restored to him.
HANNAH SMITH re-examined. This purse I bought with my own money of the prisoner some time ago for 1 s.—I afterwards gave it to my husband, and I took it from my home when I left.
NOT GUILTY .
Fifth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.

753. EMMA LEA was indicted for stealing one shawl, value 10 s., the goods of Henry Joseph Fitzgerald; and 1 petticoat, value 5 s., the goods of Helen Bruce Graville.

GEORGE BOARDMAN , ( police-constable E 46.) I received information last Monday, and went to Union-street, Middlesex Hospital, where I found the prisoner—I said I wanted her for stealing a shawl and a petticoat—she said she had taken the shawl, and gave me the duplicate of it.
CHARLOTTE HARPER . The prosecutrix lodged with me—the prisoner came to my house on the 24th of Dec. and left on the 26th.

754. FREDERICK TIEDIMAN and CHARLES WHEELER were indicted for stealing 10 paving-stones, value 5 s.; 10 other stones, value 5 s.; 10 flags, value 5 s.; and 10 feet of stone, value 5 s.; the goods of the Commissioners for carrying into execution certain Acts of Parliament. —Other COUNTS, stating them to belong to other persons.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM WELSH . I am a goldsmith, and live in Baker's-row, Clerkenwell. During the last month, the paving was being taken up in that row—on the 23rd of Jan. I saw Tiediman there between five and six o'clock in the evening—it was dusk, and he put some of the stones into a truck—after the truck was loaded, there was some one drawing it in front—I did not see Wheeler at
that time—I followed the truck, and Tiediman walked at a distance behind it—it went to a chandler's shop, No. 24, Easton-street—I saw Tiediman unload the stones, and take them in there—I gave information to the constable.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. These were old stones that had been taken up? A. I believe they were—they were lying in the middle of the street in heaps—they were not fit to use in roads, but for the foot-path—they were not broken—the pavement was up at that time about my door.
ARTHUR KEEFE . I live in St. Helena-place, Clerken well. I was employed by Mr. Stile's to repair the pavement in Baker's-row—on the 23rd of Jan. I was in Baker's-row, between five and six o'clock—Wheeler and another person brought a truck—Tiediman and Wheeler put some old pavingstones into the truck, and took it away—Tiediman ordered me to help to shove the truck up, and I did as far as No. 24, Easton-street—I had not known Wheeler before—we took the stones into the house—I did not get any thing for the job—we had a pint of beer between us.
Q. Did your master tell you where to take the stones? A. Yes, to the greenyard—that is where all the old stones are taken—I did not know when Tiediman asked me to shove where they were going to—I knew that Easton-street was not the greenyard—I saw nine or ten stones at the station—I believe they were some that had been taken from Baker's-row.
CHARLES WAUGH , butcher, Guildford-street, Clerkenwell —Wheeler is a bricklayer, and sometimes does jobs in the paving-line—on Tuesday afternoon, the 23rd of Jan., he came to me about the job of repairing my yard in Easton-street—he said he had bought some stones, and they came to 8 s. 4 1/2 d. and he had not got the money—I gave him 8 s. 6 d.,—I did not know they came from Baker's-row.
GEORGE ANDREWS ( police-constable G 66.) On the 24th of Jan. I went to Easton-street, in consequence of information, and saw Wheeler bring a paving-stone about two feet square out of the bouse No. 24, and cross the road with it on his back—I asked if it was his—he said it was—I asked where he got it from—he said, "From over the way," pointing to No. 24—I asked where he bought it—he hesitated a minute or two—Mr. Waugh came to the door, and said, "You had better tell him where you got it from"—he said, "From the back of the workhouse"—in going to the station, I asked if he had any more stones—he said, "No"—I went back to No. 24, and found nine more stones in the yard—I found Tiediman on the Friday morning in William-street, Hoxton, about five o'clock—I asked his name—he said Higgins, or Hutchins, and said he was a carpenter.
RHODES MOLES . On the 27th of Jan. I acted as clerk to the Magistrate at Clerkenwell Police-court—after the evidence had been given against the prisoners, Tiediman said, "I gave the flags to Wheeler, I had nothing for them; I had a right to give them away."—Wheeler had been in custody on the 25th, and he then said, in reference to the stone, "I bought it of one of the masons at 2 d. a foot; I paid him 6 s.; 3 s. on Monday night, and 3 s. on Tuesday evening; Keefe saw me pay the money"—on the 29th Wheeler said, "I went to Tiediman, and asked if he had any old bits of paving for sale; he said yes; I asked what he wanted a foot; he said 2 1/2 d.; I gave him 2 d. a foot; on Tuesday I had the remainder; I paid him 2 s. 10 d., and we had a pint of beer."
Cross-examined. Q. Have either of these statements been returned to this Court? A. No, they have not—neither of the prisoners signed them—when a prisoner is charged in the first instance, he is told by the Magistrate that he shall be detained in custody for examination on a subsequent day, and then he is asked if he has anything to say—the statements I have read
are what was said on that occasion—after hearing the whole evidence, if the Magistrate thinks there is a case to send to this Court, he asks what they have to say, and then what they say is always returned to this Court—there are statements made, which are not returned—no communication is made to the Court on the occasion I have stated.
Q. Is it in your knowledge that a prisoner is entitled to a copy of the deposition? A. Yes, but not of his examination, I believe—if the prisoner had come and asked for a copy of any depositions that I have, he would have had them—my practice is, when I furnish a copy of the depositions, (though I believe I am not required to do it) to give the attorney a copy of the examination also, unless I had directions not to do it.
MR. DOANE. Q. Those that you read first are not part of the depositions returned to this Court? A. No, because there are numerous cases where the prisoners are afterwards discharged, and not sent for trial—the depositions were taken on the 29th—I believe that is the course of business pursued at every Police-court.
WILLIAM STILES . I contracted with the parish, and was engaged in repairing the pavement in Baker's-row, by order of the Commissioners, on the 23rd of Jan. last—it was my duty to take the old stones to the green-yard—I see that all the stones go there as far as I can—they are kept there for various uses to repair with—stones which are too much worn for some pavements, are good enough for others—many stones taken to the green-yard are subsequently used at other places—Tiediman was engaged by me, amongst other workmen at Baker's-row—it was not his duty to take these stones to the green-yard—the work was not then completed—when it was, my carts would have taken them—Tiediman had been employed by me nine years—he would know what the practice was—that was known to all the men—Tiediman had no authority to sell them or to give them away—on the 24th of Jan. he did not come to work, nor on the 25th—he had no leave of absence from me—I did not see him again till he was at the Police-court.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose Tiediman wai your servant to work on the stones belonging to the Commissioners of Pavements? A. He was working under my directions upon the pavements—when the stones are sent to the green-yard it is for the purpose of putting them in possession of the parish for various uses—they do not become my property—these are not what I should call useless pieces.
MR. DOANE. Q. Have you seen the stones produced? A. Yes—I cannot swear to them, but they are like the stones that were there—when they are taken up they lie in the street till the work is complete—I have a contract with the Commissioners—Mr. East was the attesting witness to it.
JURY. Q. Do not the Commissioners hold you accountable for the old stones? A. My property was there at the same time—if the stones were lost, Ishould say it would be the Commissioners' loss.
WILLIAM FREDERICK EAST . I am surveyor to the Commissioners of Pavements, of St. James's, Clerkenwell. Baker's-row is in that parish—Stiles, the contractor, was engaged to repair the pavement—this written paper has nothing to do with the repair of Baker's-row—these stones are worth 10 s.—Mr. Baylie is one of the Commissioners—his name is Eli Baylie—he acts under these different acts of Parliament.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you mean he is appointed under any commission, or ny act of Parliament? A. He acts under an act of Parliament—the whole of the Commissioners do.
MR. DOANS. Q. You say Mr. Baylie is one of the Commissioners? A. Yes—they meet in Upper Rosoman-street, at the Board-room—I have seen
Mr. Bailey sitting at the Board as one of the Commissioners—I have heard him give instructions—on the last occasion when they met, he gave instructions for this prosecution—I have frequently seen him act as chairman, and he is not only our chairman, but he is chairman of the Committee of Works.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440205-755]
755. FREDERICK TIEDIMAN and CHARLES WHEELER were again indicted for stealing 10 paving stones, value 5 s.; 10 other stones, 5 s.; 10 flags, 5 s.; 10 feet of stone, 5 s.; the goods of the Commissioners for carrying into execution certain acts of Parliament.
WILLIAM WELCH . I am a goldsmith and live in Baker's-row. The pavement was being laid down near my shop, and my attention was attracted to it—on the evening of Monday, the 22nd of Jan., after it was dark, I saw Tiediman there—(he had been engaged there as a labourer in putting down the new pavement)—I saw a truck there—Tiediman filled it with paving stones—I did not see Wheeler at the time to know him—I saw the truck go away—I do not know who took it.
ARTHUR KEEFE . I was engaged in repairing Baker's-row. On the 22nd of Jan. I saw Wheeler and another young man bring a track—Wheeler and the other labourers put some of the stones into it—the other mason told me to assist in taking the truck, and I went with it to the top of Baker's-row.
( Charles Waugh and George Andrews deposed as in the former case.)
WILLIAM FREDERICK EAST . I am surveyor to the Commissioners under these Local acts—I spoke to Tiediman on the 24th of Jan.—I said the atones did not appear to increase, and requested him to relay them immediately—I meant that they appeared to decrease—Tiediman felt inclined to be impudent, and asked if I supposed he had taken them.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440205-756]
756. WILLIAM THOMPSON was indicted for stealing 2 reams of paper, value 3 l.;, the goods of John Hodge and another.
JOHN HODGE , stationer, Drury-lane. I have one partner. About three months ago I sent a quantity of paper to Mr. M'Gowan, in Windmill-street, Haymarket, by my own cart—these two reams of paper are mine.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you put it into the cart? A. No—a porter in my establishment sent it—there is no particular mark on this paper—it was made to order for a particular person—I made more than a hundred reams of it—I sent it all to Mr. M'Gowan, as it was made for as order, and not required.
DOUGAL M'GOWAN . I received some paper from Messrs. Hodge about three months ago—I am in the habit of receiving a great quantity from them to warehouse—it was of the size of this—I cannot tell whether this is any of it or not—I cannot tell the number of reams I received.
JOHN KINSMAN , stationer, Common-street. On the 8th of Jan. the prisoner came and offered me this paper—I asked how he became possessed of it—he laid he had bought it, to sell again—I bad suspicion, and stopped him.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440205-757]
757. DANIEL ALLEN was indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 4 s.; 1 pair of gloves, 6 d.; and 1 handkerchief, 6 d.; the goods of Daniel Bellinger.
DANIEL BELLINGER . I live on board the William Murray, in the East India Docks. On the night of the 3rd of Feb. I went into the Steam-ship public-house—I placed my great-coat on the back of a chair—in a few minutes I heard some person say, "That man has taken a coat"—I then heard another voice say, "Yes, and he did not bring one in with him"—I them
turned, and missed my coat—I went out directly, and saw the prisoner with it under his arm—I brought him back, and asked if he knew what he bad done—he made no reply—I had a silk handkerchief and gloves in the pocket.
Prisoner. Q. Was I drunk or sober? A. I think he had been drinking—I cannot tell whether he intended to steal it—when I asked him, he said, "I don't care anything about the coat, give me a pipe of tobacco."
Prisoner's Defence. I had several glasses of grog, and then they asked me to help a man on shore with his clothes to the Steam-ship public-house; I recollect nothing of the coat; I found myself next morning in the station.
NOT GUILTY .
NEW COURT.— Wednesday, February 14, 1844.
Fifth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.

[trialAccount t18440205-759]
759. WILLIAM CLEMITSON was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS BORRITT , butcher, King-street, Brick-lane, Spitalfields. A person named Brashier owed me 5 l.;, money lent—I have known the prisoner for twelve years—he has been in the habit of collecting money at different courts for tradesmen—seven or eight months ago I applied to him to collect this money from Mr. Brashier—I said, "William Brashier owes me 5 l.;, and the son 2 I.; I with you to try and get it; if you get it I will give you a sovereign; I do not know where they live, but the son is in the Post-office"—the prisoner told me he had inquired, and found out where Brashier lived, but he could not get the money; he must summons him—I said, "What will that cost?"—he said the 5 l.; would cost 12 s. 6 d., deposit money; and the son's 2 l.; would be 7 s. 6 d.—he brought me a little book, which he said was the Act of Parliament, I might see it—I consented, and gave him the 12 s. 6 d. and the 7 s. 6 d., about the end of July—when I saw him again, he said he had summoned them, and told me the day he was to go to the hearing—he did not bring me any money—I received a summons from Mr. Brashier—I gave the prisoner the summons and the letter from Mr. Brashier—I said, "I shall leave it all to you"—I saw him again in August, three or four days after the hearing—he said he had summoned them to the Court; the younger one was to pay 10 s. a month, and the other 9 s. a month—he had my authority to receive these sums—in the first instance I engaged him to go and get money from Brashier—I did not contemplate taking proceedings in the Court—when he came back he said there was no other way to get it—I saw him in September, and he did not then account for either of these sums—I did not see him again till he was in custody—I went to his house several times, but could not find him.,
COURT. Q. Was it his business to collect debts for different tradesmen in different courts? A. Yes, and knowing that I sent to him to collect these.
NOT GUILTY .
(There were two other indictments against the prisoner, on which no evidence was offered.)
