[trialAccount t18440205-762]
762. SAMUEL SALE was indicted for stealing 10oz. weight of leather, 11 d., the goods of Frederick Lennie.
FREDERICK LENNIE , shoemaker, High-street, Bow. The prisoner worked for me at home—I marked the materials I delivered him with an iron stamp—on the 2nd Feb. he brought me some small boots—I examined them and felt the soles of one, and found it was very soft, which it should not be, knowing the stuff I had given him—I said, "This it not filling enough"—he said it was wet—I saw a stamp that was not mine—I said, "I suspect you have been forging the mark, if I find you have I will prosecute you"—there is one pair of soles and three pair of heels which are not mine—I had given him out the materials for the shoes with my own mark—I let him have fresh work, and he went away—I looked at these again and found they were an imitation of my mark—in the evening I went with a policeman and gave him into custody—I found all the leather that was not made up there—it was worth about 10 s.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When did you give him the leather which you charge him with stealing? A. Between the 21st and the 22nd Jan. and the 2nd Feb.—I gave it out myself—I cannot say how much I gave out between those times—this pair of shoes was made up by the prisoner with my leather—this coloured leather is different leather—he had leather which in process of making would come to this colour.
COURT, Q. How many have the stamp which is not yours? A. Four pair of top pieces and one pair of soles.
JOHN PAROSE ( police-constable K 384.) I took the prisoner—he said he had not robbed his master, but at the station he said, "I admit having changed one sole, having met with an accident and burnt one of Mr. Lennie's."
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440205-763]
763. JONATHAN BARTHOLOMEW was indicted for stealing 31 live tame fowls, price 3 l.; 10 s., the property of John Forster.
WILLIAM MASTERS ( police-constable N 242.) About a quarter before three on the morning of 1st Feb. I saw the prisoner and another person in Compton-street, St. Paul's-place, I slington—there was another person about fifty yards from him—the prisoner had a bag—I asked what he had got in it—he said, "Fowls"—they had just taken them from the Cambridge wagon—I said he most go with me to the station—he said he would—he set the bag down, the other took it up and carried it some distance—they both followed me—they set them down, the third man came up, and he carried them a little way—they then put the bag down—the others ran away and left the prisoner—I took him—I found twelve dead fowls and two live ones in die bag—the dead ones were quite warm—the bag was covered with blood at the bottom—I found on the prisoner a knife, and a piece of rope and some feathers in his hat, and a handkerchief—he said he had been to see a shipmate, and in returning met these men who asked if he would earn 1 s., they took him to the bottom of a lane and told him to wait there, and they brought these things to him to carry.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. This third man was watching? A.
Yes—the second man raised a dispute about carrying the bag, and then he made off—the next day I found the feathers in the prisoner's hat.
MATTHEW HEAD . I am gardener to Mr. John Forster. In the evening of Wednesday, the 31st of Jan., he had forty-one fowls safe in his shed—I locked the door, and left the fowls safe—next morning, about seven o'clock, I missed thirty-one—the padlock was still there, but the door was brokei open—I saw fourteen of the fowls at the station that day—I could swear to them.
CHARLES FORDER . I am coachman to Mr. John Forster. On the 24th of Jan., between one and two o'clock, I saw the prisoner and another person in the lane near where the fowls were kept—they appeared to me to be feeding the fowls—we missed a big Spanish cock that day—I am certain the prisoner is one of the persons.
Q. I suppose you never made a mistake in your life? A. I do not know that—I swear I saw this prisoner there—I never mistook one man for another.
Witness for the Defence.
THOMAS WYATT , wine-cooper, Bethnal-green-road. I have known the prisoner eight years—he works as a porter in the City—on the 24th of Jan. I saw him in the City, from ten o'clock in the morning till half-past nine at night—he was engaged at Mr. Rowe's, in King William-street, and carried several things for them—I was at the same place, and saw him off and on, backwards and forwards—between one and two in the day he and I were in the public-house getting our dinner.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 32.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18440205-764]
764. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing 5 deal-boards, value 1 l.; 5 s.; and 12 feet of quartering, value 2 s., the goods of James Dobbins.
JAMES DOBBINS , ironmonger, Chelsea. I have a building at Kensington, and employed different men—I had a great quantity of deal-boards there at the latter end of Nov.—on the 24th of Jan. the policeman took the prisoner—I saw some boards at the Police-office—they were mine, to the best of my, knowledge—one I could positively swear to—I had ft in my hand, and had used a part of it before I brought it to that building—I lost a great quantity of quartering, and seventy-five flooring-boards from the building.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did you employ the prisoner to do the plastering of some skeleton houses? A. Yes, in Kensington New-town—I think I engaged him about Aug. or Sept.—he was to find the scaffolding and the boards that he was to stand on—he was at work at this house at different times—the policeman gave me the first information about this—the prisoner was taken, and I went before the Magistrate at Queen-square—five dealboards were produced by the policeman—the prisoner was charged with stealing them—I gave my evidence—I did not tell the Magistrate that the prisoner came twice to my house to beg pardon—I said I could swear to one of these boards—the Magistrate dismissed the case, and ordered the property to be given up to the prisoner on the second occasion—he said, first, that I might have them, and on the second occasion he said the prisoner might have them—he was suffered to go at large without any bail or recognizance—I then went before the same Magistrate again—he said I had better go to a higher Court—I then went before the Grand Jury, and found a bill—I came up here with the policeman—I had conversation with him about the boards before I went before the Grand Jury—I know I had these five boards in my possession
in Nov.—they had been there long before that, and had been planed up by the carpenter—I saw the whole of them there in Nov.
JOHN CHERRY , labourer. The prisoner was my employer—he and I went for materials in Jan. last—we brought them from one job, and put them to another—I did not know who the boards belonged to—I brought them from a house belonging to the prosecutor in Kensington New-town, on the 24th or 25th of Jan., and put them in Pelham-cottage, Pelham-road, belonging to Mr. Turner—the prisoner was engaged in both these places—I do not know how many boards there were—they were used for scaffolding.
Cross-examined. Q. Were they boards that plasterers used to stand on? A. Yes—he did not tell me not to say anything about them—he might have taken them himself.
RICHARD HALL . On Saturday, the 27th of Jan., the prosecutor sent for me to go to the station to see if I knew these boards—I knew them to be the prosecutor's boards—I do not know when they were safe, or when they were missed.
Cross-examined. Q. When had you seen them, before the 27th of Jan.? A. When I was working at the house two months ago—I am sure I saw them in Nov.—I saw them in the house, and concluded they were the prosecutor's, and I had the fellow ones planed up.
JAMES BRADLY ( police-constable B 134.) I took the prisoner at a quarter-past five o'clock in the evening of the 25th—he had this piece of board, and his son had another piece—they were carrying them towards his home.
JAMES DOBBINS re-examined. I know this is part of mine—I should not like to swear to it.
JOHN SKIPPER , builder. Henry-place. Chelsea. I contracted with the owner to do the carpenter's and joiner's work where this timber was lost—I saw the timber in the basement story on Thursday, the 25th of Jan.—the prisoner said he had a board that I might cut into a rule—I said I did not wish to cut other person's property—I identified one of the boards, which laid with the lot which the prisoner claimed.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440205-765]
765. WILLIAM FANN was indicted for stealing 1 saw, value 5 s., the goods of George Greenfield; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
GEORGE GREENFIELD . I keep a coal-shed in Clerkenwell-close. On Wednesday evening, the 31st of Jan., I was at work in my shed—I saw the prisoner and two little boys behind him—I saw the prisoner take hold of a saw, which belonged to my son George, and hung against the door—I told him to go away—I turned round to put a candle on a piece of wood, and when I turned again, he and the saw were gone.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me take it? A. I saw you take hold of it, and told you to go away.
EDWARD MACKLIN . I Know the prisoner—I saw him on the evening of the 31st of Jan., between five and six o'clock, coming from towards the prosecutor's with a bow-saw on his left arm—I knew the saw before—I could not swear it was the same—it was very like it.
Prisoner. When he saw me it was between seven and eight o'clock; I was with Mr. Baker, going to have his saw sharpened in Old-street. Witness. The prisoner had the saw—there was another man walking on the curb side, with nothing on his arm.
Witness for the Defence.
JOHN BAKER . I am a wood-cutter, and live in Clerkenwell-close. I was walking with the prisoner when Macklin met him this day fortnight, in the
evening—I had this saw with me, which I have here now—it is my own property—the prisoner had nothing with him—it was about seven o'clock.
GEORGE PUTNAM . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—I was present at the trial—the prisoner is the person.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18440205-766]
766. ROBERT WRIGHT was indicted for stealing 5 quires of coloured paper, value 5 s.; 2 quires of printed paper, 1 s.; 2 iron chases for printing, 2 s.; 1lb. weight of type, 1 s.; and 11 pressing-boards, 11 s.; the goods of George John Ford.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE JOHN FORD , printer, Beech-street, Barbican. I live at Holloway—my time for coming to my business, which is in Barbican, is uncertain—I had four apprentices and a young man—I have been obliged to take proceedings against the whole of them—I have known the prisoner for years—he is a bookbinder and card-maker, in Brick-lane, St. Luke's—in consequence of information, I accompanied the policeman to the prisoner's house, on Sunday, the 4th of Feb.—I there found five quires of coloured paper, two quires of printed paper, and two iron chases, or frames, for what we call locking up the type—I know them to be mine—I saw them safe two or three months ago—there was 1 lb. weight of type—I believe it to form part of my type—I have missed type—I also found eleven pressing-boards, which are mine—I know them well—part of them were my father's—I have never sold them—I missed such paper as this, and it has the mark of our printing on some of it—it was printed wrong, and was returned.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did all your four apprentices come out of the workhouse? A. No, only three—I do not eat flesh—I eat grain and fruit, and drink only water—I am what is called an "Healthian," and am of the Society of Friends—my apprentices had things to live upon that other people did, by approval of the parish authorities—they began to rob me about eleven months ago—they complained of their food—I did not give them grain and fruit—I have had Franklin with me about three years—he gave evidence against Jeffries the other night—the whole of this paper was found at the prisoner's house as it is now, cut up—it was mixed up with other paper which has been taken from-the printing-office, but I identify it by the printing mark on it—I can speak positively to these chases, by the marks in the casting of them—here is "No. 1, J. T." on this one, and here is a fault in this one—these boards have worm-holes in them, and are worth 10 s. or 12 s.—this type is just what we missed from our lot, and is the same sort we have—here are two different sorts mixed together—I do not suppose that two printers in London have the same type.
MR. DOANE. Q. How long have you had these chases? A. Three or four years—I was in the constant habit of using them—I have no doubt that they are mine.
JOHN BENJAMIN FRANKLIN . I am apprentice to the prosecutor. I have known the prisoner about eleven months—he once had some work—we took it to him—he afterwards came to my master's shop—the eldest apprentice showed him some paper, and about a week after I showed him some—he said, "Oh, by George, that will do nicely to make cards of," and said if I could get him some more he would be very much obliged to us—I have been charged with robbing my master, and sent to gaol—the following night we got the paper ready for him, and the next morning he came and took it in his apron—he
took these two iron chases one morning from the office, and, the same day, a composing-stick, which has not been found.
Cross-examined. Q. You are seventeen years old, are you not? A. Yes—all four of us agreed to rob our master—it is about three months ago that the prisoner took the chases and the composing-stick—Jeffreys came into my master's service about seven months ago—it was two or three months before that that I began to rob my master—it was not two or three yean—I did not say any thing to my master till I was sent to prison, and had got out; then my master spoke to me, and I told him I would tell him the truth.
HENRY DAVID LLOYD , printer, George's-place, Holloway. I accompanied the prosecutor, on Sunday the 4th, to the prisoner's residence—I found these iron chases under the staircase in the passage to the shop—he said he was not aware of their being there—they must have been placed there by the boys unknown to him—I went into the passage to the staircase, and these chases were quite under the stairs—it was a place you might pass 100 time and not know it.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 66.— Confined Six Months.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18440205-767]
767. JOHN CANNON was indicted for embezzlement.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES PARR . I am porter at the Eastern Counties Railway-station, at Shoreditch. There is a class of fares called "excess fares"—it was my duty to take them on the 28th of Dec.—on the arrival of the luggage train from Colchester, I received for three persons and a child 14 s. for excess fares—I paid it over to the prisoner immediately, and produced one of the party who had come up as acting-guard to the prisoner at the time—I received the 4 s. from that person—I told the prisoner the particulars of these receipts.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. That 4 s. was paid in the office? A. Yes, I took the person into the superintendent's and inspector's office, who was acting-guard to Salter's wagon—I paid the prisoner 10 s. first, sod then I received the 4 s. from that other man in the office, which I handed over to the prisoner—I had received the 10 s. on the line, but the 4 s. was paid in the office in the prisoner's presence, and by me to him—I have been about six months in the company's service—I had been in the police before, and had been in the army.
JOHN NIGHTINGALE HOTSON . I am chief clerk at the London-station of the Railway—the excess fares are created by persons not having taken tickets, or losing their tickets—it was the inspector's duty to pay these excess fares to me—the prisoner was an inspector—if a sum was paid to him on the 28th of Dec; it was his duty to pay it me by nine o'clock the next morning—he did not pay me 10 s. or 4 s.—he has not paid this since.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you do not know whether he was on duty on the 28th? A. No—there are other inspectors besides him—they take turns—Pocock was there occasionally—the prisoner was busily engaged when he was there—it was the Christmas week—mistakes will occur in the hurry of business, which was about the same at that time as it is in the summer season—this is the book—here is 4 s. put down as paid to me on the 29th of Dec—in most cases that would show that it was paid to the prisoner on the 28th, but it was not so in this case—here is the prisoner's memorandum to show it was not—he has inserted on the document 4 s. on the 29th, and if this was for money received on the 28th, it ought to have been dated the 28th—I bave no document of money received on the 28th—this was paid on the 29th),
but trains arrive sometimes before he pays me, and this sum might have been received previous to that morning—I have no means of ascertaining whether it had been paid on the 29th.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Does it appear by this document that this was from the luggage train? A. No, it appears merely as excess fares—this is the paper given to me by the prisoner on the 29th, and at that time he paid over 4 s.—in the book here is a sum of 4 s. received for one man from Chelmsford, by the goods train, on the 29th, he having no ticket.
WILLIAM STEVENSON M'INTYRE . I am inspector of the Eastern Counties Railway. It is the duty of the inspector to receive excess fares in case of the sickness of the porter or the policeman.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know any thing of a man coming by the goods train from Chelmsford, on the morning of the 29th of Dec.? A. No.
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18440205-770]
770. THOMAS BROADLE was indicted for conspiracy.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
ABRAHAM BERLYN . In Nov. 1840, I was living in Back Church-lane, Whitechapel, and carried on the business of a boot and shoemaker. After the fire at Hoare's-wharf, I purchased some Hessian linen canvas, and other things, at the sales—there were five successive sales—I saw a man named Nathan at all the sales, and he saw me—on the 4th of Nov., Nathan came to me at Goulston-street, where I have a warehouse—he said to me, "Well, Mr. Berlyn, have you sold all your linen yet?"—I said, "No"—he said, "I think I can sell a great quantity for you; what will you allow me?"—I said, "I will allow you 5 l.; per cent. on all that you sell for me"—he said 5 l.; per cent, would not pay him, and it was ultimately agreed that I should allow him a farthing a yard, which made it come to 6 1/4 l.; per cent—he asked me to give him samples of the various sorts I had, and the quantity of each, which I did—he came again the same day and said he had seen the person he wanted the linen for, that he was captain of his own vessel, and a man of large property, that the captain wished to see me himself to bargain for this linen, and if I would go with him at half-past four that afternoon, after 'Change time, to No. 15, Baker-street, Lloyd's-square, Captain Cornish would try to deal with me—we arranged to go that afternoon at half-past four, after 'Change time—about twelve o'clock Nathan came again and said he had seen the captain since he saw me, and the captain wanted a quantity of flock—I said, "Flock is a thing I do not sell, and I am no judge of it"—he said, "I could not haw come to a better person than you; I know you have a few pounds at command; I know a person who has a large quantity on hand, and has a bill to pay, and he would be glad to sell it at a little loss to turn it into cash"—I said I
was no judge of it—he said he had been brought up in that business all his life, and he knew the value of it to a fraction—he advised me to take a sample of it to the captain—Nathan went and got the sample and brought me the price as 25 l.; a ton—he said the man had a ton and a half of it—Nathan and I then took the samples of the linen and the flock to No. 15, Baker-street, Lloyd's-square—the name of Cornish was on the door—we knocked, a lady opened the door, Nathan went in and I followed him—the lady said, "Your pleasure, sir?"—he said, "Will you be kind enough to tell Captain Cornish that Mr. Nathan is come?"—the parlour door then opened, and the prisoner was there—he said, "It is you, Mr. Nathan; step in"—we went into the parlour, and when we entered Nathan bowed to the prisoner, and introduced me as Mr. Berlyn, and then he said to me, "This is Captain Cornish"—the prisoner asked us to sit down, and when we had sat a minute or two, the prisoner laid, "Well, sir, I understand you have a quantity of this linen?"—he asked me the price—I said I had 4,000 yards of the Hessian linen, at 4 d., and 4,000, at 3 1/4 d.—he offered me 3 d. a yard, and to take the whole 8000 yards—I told him I could not make any abatement from the price, and he said as that was the case he could only take 1000 yards at 4 d.—when that bargain was made he said, "Have you brought a pattern of that flock that I wanted, Mr. Nathan?"—Nathan said, "Yes, Mr. Berlyn has got it"—I produced it, and agreed to let the captain have it at 30 l.; a ton—I told him there was about a ton and a half—he said he wanted it for shipping—he did not mention what vessel he was captain of, but said he was going to set sail in a day or two—when I asked him the price for the flock he said I was much cheaper with the flock than I was with the linen, and he agreed to take that at once—he said I ought to make some little allowance, as all his dealings were for cash—I said I did business on no other terms—he then said, "I want these goods to-morrow; they must be here between twelve and one"—I said very well, he should have them, and they were to be paid for on delivery—that was the agreement between us at the time—I was then about to take my departure, and the captain said, "Won't you take a glass of wine before you go?"—I said I was not in the habit of drinking wine—he said I was not half a sailor, and would I have some grog—I at last had a drop of clear gin—I then went away, and Nathan with me—I laid to him, "Where is this flock?"—he told me to come with him, and took me to a man named Isaacs, of whom I bought the flock for 35 l.;—the next day I took the flock and linen in a van and a cart—I paid a sovereign for the use of them—I went, and Nathan with me, to the house in Baker-street—I knocked at the door, and the captain opened it himself—he said, "Good morning, Mr. Berlyn; you have brought these goods; you are very exact; if you will be kind enough to take them round to my warehouse, in Spring-street, my man will be there and receive them of you, and he will assist you in unloading; then come back, and I will pay you"—I was going to leave the house with the goods, and he said to me, "Have you got the invoice?"—I said, "Yes"—he said, "Let me peruse it, to see that it is all right"—I gave it him and went round to Spring-street, where I met his man, and he assisted me to unload the goods into a coach-house—I then gave the men a sovereign for the van, and 1 s. to get some bread and cheese—I went back to the house, and knocked—I saw a woman, but I could not find the captain," and could not get my money—the next day I went to the police-office, and, by the advice of the Magistrate, I was accompanied to the prisoner's house by the police-inspector—I went to the warehouse with a sledge-hammer, and gave two or three men 1 s. a-piece to assist me—I broke open the door—I found the flock, and got all that back again—I went across the yard, and got into the house—I saw a lad, who came with a crow-bar, and said, "What do you want here?" and made a blow at me—I did not see the prisoner there at first, but I saw him afterwards at the back parlour window—he said nothing—I said, "I want my property"—a woman who was there held up the invoice, in the prisoner's presence, and said, "What do you want? it is only a mere debt; you can do nothing but sue for it"—I had broken the window to get into the house, followed by two or three persons—I got severely handled by a number of persons in the house where the prisoner was—one struck me with a poker in the ribs, and I did not recover it for three weeks, and I got nearly blinded, as I had an umbrella shoved into my eye—I found about half the linen in the house, and succeeded in gaining that—the persons who went with me were frightened, and decamped—the value of what I lost was about 58 l.;—I saw Nathan after that, and taxed him with being a party concerned—he said what a fool I must have been to be gulled by him out of so much money, and he only wanted such fools as me to enable him to pay for his wife's confinement—he mentioned something about sharing some money, but I forget it almost; it was, that two or three of them had a share, or a swag, or something, out of it—I once saw the captain afterwards, but I could not secure him; I had broken both my legs and arms, and could not chase him—I found him here in Newgate, and recognized him at once.
Prisoner. I have had witnesses to prove that I was not in the house when you brought the goods, nor when you took them away; did you ever see me in the house after you brought the samples? Witness. Yes, I saw you in the room.
MR. DOANE. Q. Is he the man who was introduced to you by Nathan in the first instance? A. Yes, and who bargained with me for the quantity, who received the goods, and told me to take them round to the warehouse—I have not the slightest doubt of it.
Prisoner. Q. Did you hear me say I was Captain Cornish? A. Mr. Nathan introduced you to me as Captain Cornish.
COURT. Q. Where were you introduced, and when Nathan said, "Mr. Berlyn, Captain Cornish," did he say anything? A. Nathan said, "This is Captain Cornish,"and I bowed and said, "Captain Cornish, I presume?" and he said, "Yes"—he said he was a captain, and wanted to ship the goods—the invoice was made out to Captain Cornish, and I gave it into his hands—he saw it, but did not say anything.
WILLIAM PENNY . I am an inspector of the G division of police. Mr. Berlyn complained of having been swindled out of his property, and in consequence of directions, I accompanied him to No. 15, Baker-street—I stood at the front door while he went round to the warehouse—I did not see any body—I did not go into the yard—I have known the prisoner many years, efer since I have been in the police—I cannot say that I knew him by the name of Cornish—that name was on the door of the house—I knew him by the name of Broad—he lived within a few yards of the station—I have also known him by the name of Brown.
THOMAS FIELD . I am a baker, and live in Thurlow-place, Hackney-road. I knew the prisoner when he lived in Northampton-place, Hackney-road, from Oct. 1835 to Dec. 1835—I knew him by the name of Thomas Broad—I knew him when residing in Goldsmith-place—I think that was in 1839—he then went by the name of Eden—I saw him in 1842 at No. 7, Philadelphia-place, which is a very large house, one of the largest in the road—I expressed my
surprise to him as to how he got into so large a place—he said, "I have got a house twice as large, and I have got a reference now in the City that will beat the devil."
Prisoner. I dealt with you? Witness. Yes—you did not pay me for all you had.
Prisoner's Defence. It is all false about their talking of Captain Cornish; I never saw the prosecutor only when he brought the samples; they thought to take me in with the rubbish, they came in the back way, and took every thing out; I never saw it come in nor go out.
GUILTY . Aged 63.— Confined Eighteen Months.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)

[trialAccount t18440205-774]
774. WILLIAM BRACEY was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of Jan., 55 lbs. weight of hay, value 1 s. 6 d.; and three bushels of a mixture of chaff and bran, value 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Samuel Ball, his master.
SAMUEL BALL . The prisoner was in my employ at East Ham. On the 18th of Jan. I was with him in the stable, and ordered him to come to town with a load of dung with three horses and a cart, and told him to fill three nose-bags, which contain nearly a bushel a piece of chaff and bran mixed—the boy filled them—he was to start at three o'clock in the morning—I told Stacey to look out when he left.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Had you had any quarrel with the prisoner the night before? A. I have had a few words with him at different times—he did not upset a cart of dung into a ditch—he got wrong, and I was angry with him—I was called up about half-past three o'clock in the morning.
GEORGE STACEY ( police-constable K 269.) I stopped the prisoner with Mr. Ball's cart about half-past three o'clock in the morning, about a hundred yards from Mr. Ball's—I asked what he had in the cart—he said, two baskets, and a little bundle of hay for his horses—I called my brother constable, who got up into the cart, and told me there was a truss of hay there—I then took the prisoner back, called Mr. Balls ip, and asked if he had allowed the prisoner to take any hay—he said, "No"—the prisoner beard that—on examining the cart again, I found hanging on one of the shafts, a bag, containing bran and chaff—the nose-bags which were on the foreladder of the cart were full—I asked Mr. Ball to go up in his loft, and see if he missed any hay—he did so, and said the truss he had seen there overnight was gone.
Cross-examined. Q. Your brother officer who found the hay is not here, is he? A. No, but I saw him find it, and chuck it from the cart into the
road—I was standing against the wheel—it has not been in my custody ever since—I delivered it to the inspector at Ilford station, but I put a mark on it with sealing-wax—I saw the prisoner about twenty minutes or half an hour before we started—he asked me what time it was, and I told him three o'clock—he saw me and the other officer also—he was admitted to bail, and has surrendered to-day.
SAMUEL BALL re-examined. I looked in my loft overnight, and counted seven trusses of hay, and in the morning I missed one—I saw the hay the policeman found, and have no doubt it is mine—it is the same quality—I had not authorised him to take any—the mixture in the bag was similar to what I allowed him to take in the nose-bags.
Cross-examined. Q. It is a very deep cart, is it not? A. Yes—he started between three and four o'clock in the morning—I told him to take the baskets to carry the dung in.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440205-775]
775. EDWARD RUMBLE and JOSEPH FRANCIS were indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 10 s., the goods of Levi William Bartlett.
LEVI WILLIAM BARTLETT . I am a carrier to Wickford, in Essex. On the 25th of Jan. I was returning from London with my cart and two horses—I stopped at the Rabbits, at Ilford, about half-past ten o'clock at night—my son was with me—I went into the public-house, leaving my coat on the hind ladder of the cart underneath the tilt—I sent my son out to see that the horses and things were all right, and when I went out I missed my coat-while I was in the public-house the prisoners came in together—they asked the landlord to trust them with a pot of beer—he said he would not, and they had nothing—I do not recollect seeing any one else there.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Who asked for the pot of beer? A. Francis—I never saw them before—I did not hear Rumble ask for a pot of beer—Francis asked him to trust him—the landlord appeared to know him.
LEVI RICHARD BARTLETT . I was at the Rabbits with my father—he sent me out to have an eye to the cart—I went out twice, and the third time the prisoners went out before me—I followed them outside the door—I stopped there, and when they got to the hind ladder of the cart they stopped about two minutes, and then went down towards Ilford gaolway.
Cross-examined. Q. How far were you from them? A. About two yards—I did not see what they were doing—they seemed both together—I could just see their white flannel jackets—they did not return.
GEORGE STACEY ( police-constable K 269.) On Thursday night, the 25th of Jan., I was on duty in Little Ilford parish, and passed the Rabbits—I received a description of a coat said to be lost—I went to the house of Rumble's father with sergeant Collier—Collier went up stairs, and when he came down he produced this coat to me, which answered the description I received—I went with Collier to Wanstead, and took the prisoners.
Cross-examined. Q. Does Rumble live at his father's? A. I cannot tell—the prisoners were both together when we took them—they were digging in a gentleman's garden with six or seven others, about two miles and a half from the Rabbits—I believe Francis lives in a little cottage in Mr. Amsden's yard.
CHARLES COLLIER ( police-sergeant K 30.) I found this coat on a bed up stairs, which Rumble's father said was the prisoner's—I went with Stacey, and apprehended the prisoners.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know where Francis lives? A. Yes—he
does not live at Ramble's—I am not aware that any other labourers live at Rumble's, only his brothers.
EDWARD RUMBLE . The prisoner Rumble is my son—this coat was delivered to the officer in my presence—I cannot say how it came there—I was in bed before my son came home—I left the door ajar for him—I cannot say at what time he came home, as I had been to sleep, but I think between eleven and twelve o'clock—the coat was not there overnight when I went to bed—I was in bed when my son came home—my two little boys slept in the same bed with him.
Cross-examined. Q. How far is your house from the Rabbits? A. Better than a quarter of a mile—one of the children is fourteen years old, and the other ten—they were in bed before me, and I went between eight and nine—I cannot rightly recollect whether any body else was in the house that evening.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Rumble's Defence. I know nothing at all about the coat.
RUMBLE — GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Three Months.
FRANCIS — NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

777. EDWARD HAWKINS was indicted for stealing 1 tame rabbit, price 1 s. 6 d.; the property of William Poole; and that he had been before convicted of felony; to which he pleaded

[trialAccount t18440205-778]
778. SAMUEL PETTIT was indicted for stealing 3 tame pigeons, price 5 l.; the property of Henry Clarke.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY CLARKE . I am a brushmaker, and live at Walthamstow—I keep pigeons in a loft over a stable, and a wash-house. On Monday morning, the 22nd of Jan., I fed them, and saw they were all safe—there were from sixty to seventy—on the Tuesday morning, between seven and eight o'clock, I found the lattice door of the loft and the area door broken—the person had got over the pigstye—there were thirty pigeons missing—they were valuable—the prisoner had come to my house for employment five or six weeks before—he, wished to build a shed for me, but I did not allow him to do so—he came on my premises at that time, and saw the pigeons, and remarked to my man that they were very fine ones—on the Friday after the robbery I found four of my pigeons at Mr. Puryer's, in Hare-street, Bethnal-green—he described to me the person from whom he had them—in consequence of that I went with a police-sergeant to the prisoner's house, at No. 11, Hope-terrace, Bow-common, and in a back bed-room found fifteen of my pigeons—I then went into a shed in the yard, and out of a number of pigeons which were there, I selected three, which are all marked, and I can swear they are mine—this is one of them—it is a red-beard, and is marked on the wing—here is another which is marked on the wing—another, with a white feather in the tail, which was marked by myself—here is another, which if called an almond-drag, which is not to be matched in London—I value it at a guinea.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Had you other persons about the house when the prisoner called on you? A. There was only one person—it is seldom that persons come there—I only showed my pigeons to gentlemen, and persons who I thought would not come and steal them.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. On what occasion was it the prisoner came to you? A. To kill some rats, which were troublesome to my pigeons—he stood on the bank and looked into the pigeon-loft.
ROBERT PURYER . I live in Hare-street, Bethnal-green, and deal in pigeons—I have known the prisoner since Christmas last. On the 25th of Jan. he brought me four pigeons, which I gave him 3 s. 10 d. for—one of them is here—they were fancy pigeons, but of no value—we could get plenty of them at Is. a piece—he asked me to buy some more, and I bought three more for halt-a-crown.
Cross-examined. Q. You bought them readily? A. Yes—I knew he was a fancier of pigeons—he has bought of me, and has sold me some.
JAMES M'CULLOCH ( police-sergeant N 36.) On the 26th of Jan. I accompanied the prosecutor to Bow—I examined the prisoner's house, and found the pigeons—I afterwards examined the shoes he had on his feet—I had examined some foot-marks at Mr. Clarke's premises, and in my opinion they corresponded with the prisoner's shoes.
JOHN ANDREWS . I am a baker, and live at Low Layton—there is a public-house called the Blackbird, about a mile and a half from Mr. Clarke's—I saw the prisoner at that public-house, about five o'clock on the Monday evening before the robbery, and saw him again about eight the same evening, at the same house—I knew him, and have not the least doubt of his person.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined One Year.
KENT CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18440205-779]
779. WILLIAM SMITH HOLMES was indicted for stealing 25 yards of printed cotton, value 12 s., the goods of Henry Goodwin.
HENRY GOODWIN , linen-draper, Deptford. The cotton now produced it mine—I saw it at my door on 4th Jan.—I afterwards missed it and saw it in the hands of the officer.
THOMAS HARRIS ( police-constable R 137.) On 4th Jan. I was on duty in High-street, Deptford, and saw the prisoner walking up and down High-street—he stopped at the corner of Union-street, by Mr. Goodwin's shop, and Isaw him take this print down from inside the shop—I followed him and found it on him.
Prisoner. He saw me take it from a female's hand, a Miss Grey, who owed me some money; she said she would go to Mr. Goodwin's, get a shawl on trust, as she had done before, pledge it, and pay me part of what she owed me. I begged of the officer to go into the shop, as the lady was there thai gave it me, but he would not. I was very tipsy. She brought it to the door and gave it into my hand. Witness, I did not see any female, or anybody but the prisoner—I was noticing him for five minutes before—there were two or three pieces hanging there—he felt the quality of each, and took this off the line, put it under bis coat and walked across the road nearly to where I was standing in the dark—he appeared quite sober—he did not ask me to go
into the shop, or inquire for the woman that gave it him—he said nothing of that kind till next day—I was standing thirty or forty yards from the shop—I never went to find Miss Grey.
HENRY GOODWIN re-examined. The prisoner said nothing about Miss Grey at the station—not till next morning before the Magistrate—he appeared sober—I do not know Miss Grey—the prisoner requested me to ask my assistant if he knew her—he is not here.
Prisoner's Defence. Miss Grey had been a lodger of mine, and owed me 14 l.;, she paid me in dribblets; Mr. Goodwin told me he had made inquiries of his man, and there were two women in the shop at this time. I am an upholsterer at Speilinghurst, near Tonbridge-wells; I came to Deptford for some money owing to me. The inspector sent the policeman out, he was away two hours, and when he returned he said there was such a person in the New-town, but he could not find her; an officer there said he could find her directly. The officer speaks very falsely; I received the shawl from Miss Grey's hand.
GUILTY . Aged 54.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18440205-780]
780. NATHANIEL KING was indicted for stealing 2 tame pheasants, price 35 s.; 6 tame pigeons, 12 s.; 7 tame fowls, 14 s.; 2 ducks, 5 s.; and 9 pears, 6 d., the property of Thomas Perronet Thompson.
JOSIAH VINCENT ( police-constable R 196.) On Monday morning the 8th Jan., about half-past two o'clock, I was on duty in Church-street, Deptford, and saw the prisoner crossing Church-street, on the footpath of the railway, towards town, with a large bundle at hit back—being an unseasonable hour I went after him and asked what he had in the bundle—he said that he was a servant, that he had just left his situation, and had got his dirty clothes; that he had met with a friend and had taken a glass—I asked if they were his own—he said, "Yes"—I took him to the station, and found the bundle contained two live pheasants, four live pigeons, two dead ones, seven dead fowls, and two dead ducks, and no dirty clothes—I showed the things to Waterhouse.
Prisoner. Q. What distance was I from you when you first saw me? A. About thirty yards.
JOSEPH WATERHOUSE . I am gardener to Col. Thomas Perronet Thompson—he had some pheasants, pigeons, fowls, and ducks, safe on the evening of the 7th Jan.—I missed them between eight and nine o'clock on the 9th—I have since seen them in Vincent's hands, and know them to be the same—they were kept in the stable and loft—the stable was not locked.
Prisoner. Q. When I lived with Col. Thompson, were these things kept in the stable? A. In the summer they run loose, but in winter they were closed—I forget whether Col. Thompson had pheasants while you were in his service—the fowls used to be locked in the hen-house, and the key taken to a drawer kept by young Mr. Thompson—I can identify all the poultry as my master's—I know the pheasants by a ring round their necks, and the cock pheasant had lost its tail—they are English ring pheasants—I am quite sure of the fowls and ducks—the yard-gate was open till ten o'clock—I do not know whether it was locked after that.
Prisoner's Defence. I left Col. Thompson's service about two years ago (having lived with him four years), since which I have been in another situation. On the 6th Jan. I was out of place, and went to Woolwich to inquire among the tradespeople if one was vacant; I staid there till past twelve, and it being a fine night I thought I would walk home, and as I came across the
heath I saw a person drop something from his shoulder and walk away towards Greenwich; I went up, and it was the bag produced, containing the pheasants and poultry; I brought it on through Greenwich, intending to give it to a policeman, and when I got near Deptford station the policeman called to me, and thinking he might suppose I had stolen the things I told a falsehood, not knowing what to do. If I had intended to escape I could easily have done so, as I was thirty yards from him.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 42.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Four Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18440205-781]
781. WILLIAM LADD was indicted for stealing 10 lbs. weight of beef, value 5 s., the goods of John Knapp.
JOHN KNAPP . I am a butcher, and live at Deptford. On the 31st of Jan., about eight o'clock in the evening, I went home with some meat, and when I returned I missed a piece of beef from the salting-tray, in the window—the prisoner came into the shop, after I had missed it, for half a pound of sausages—he said he had 30,000 l.; coming to him, and he should be a good customer—I followed him about fifty yards up the street—he stooped, and took up something, near two uninhabited houses—he then ran—I ran and took him—he dropped this piece of beef out of his hand, and laid hold of me by the throat—I called a policeman, and gave him into custody—I am sure the beef he dropped was mine.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me take it out of your shop? A. No—I saw you pick something up—you went on running—I came up, and said, "Are you going to Deptford?"—you said, "Yes, go on, I will be with you"—I then took you, and you dropped this beef.
Prisoner. A man took me on board a ship, and the captain gave me some rum and water; I then went and bought some sausages; I was not sober; I was going along, and saw something white against the houses, and I took it up.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

782. GEORGE CHAPMAN and JOHN DURANT were indicted for stealing 50 yards of gingham, value 12 s. 6 d., the goods of Henry Goodwin.
HENRY GOODWIN , linen-draper, High-street, Deptford. I had some gingham, at six o'clock in the evening of the 4th of Jan., about six feet within the doorway—I missed'it about eight o'clock—this is it.
JOHN WHITE ( police-constable R 180.) On the 4th of Jan. I saw the two prisoners, a boy not in custody, and two females, by Greenwich Church—I followed them about a mile, to Deptford—they stood opposite the prosecutor's shop—the two females turned down Union-street—Durant then crossed the road, and left the others at the prosecutor's shop—the little boy then went into the shop—he took two pieces of gingham, and handed them over to Chapman—Durant stood on the opposite side of the way, and whistled—he was in sight of the shop.
Chapman's Defence. My father sent me to Deptford; a man stopped me, and asked me to carry this to Tooley-street.
Durant's Defence. I saw Chapman standing opposite a window; he said, "Wait a minute, I am coming your way; "I turned down Collins-street, and was taken.
(Chapman received a good character.)
CHAPMAN — GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Three Months.
DURANT — GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Four Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

783. ANN WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing 3 lbs. weight of mutton, value 1 s., the goods of William Couldry; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN JENNINGS . I am in the service of Wm. Couldry, a batcher at Greenwich. A little after seven o'clock, on Thursday evening, the 8th of Feb., the prisoner was going by the shop—she stopped at the corner of the board first, then at the middle of the board, took a breast of mutton, and walked away with it—I followed, and saw her cover her shawl over it, and slip it under her arm—it fell on the ground, and I stopped her.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How long have you been in the batcher line? A. About six months—my master was in the room, having his tea—the prisoner appeared to have been drinking—my master was willing to let her go, and the officer did not like to do that—the mutton was outside the window.
JOHN GRIFFIN ( police-constable R 229.) I took the prisoner—I saw the mutton on the pavement—I asked how she came to take it—she said she did not care a d—n—she was the worse for liquor.
MICHAEL THOMAS CANNON ( police-constable R 157.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY. Aged 42.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Three Months.
SURREY CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.

784. CHARLES ELLIOTT was indicted for stealing 6 cheroots, value 1 s., the goods of Mary Phoebe Taylor Williams.
MARY PHCEBE TAYLOR WILLIAMS . I keep a tobacconist's-shop in Blackfriars-road. On Saturday, the 6th of Jan., about seven in the evening, the prisoner came and asked how many cheroots I would let him have for 1 s.—I said six—he asked if I would not let him have seven—I said no—he said he would take a shilling's-worth—I counted them out, and laid them on the counter—he tried to change one, it being chipped—I would not allow that—I laid them on one side, to wrap them up—he snatched them up, threw down a bad shilling, and ran out—I do not give credit—I could not prevent his snatching them up, he did it while I turned—I laid the shilling on the counter while my brother ran after him—it was there about five minutes—my brother looked at it when be returned without the prisoner—I then threw it into a drawer where I had two other bad shillings, two half-crowns, and a sixpence—I gave the policeman all the bad shillings I had from that drawer—I saw it was a bad shilling before he got out, and called out, "This is a bad one," loud enough for him to hear—I cannot swear which of the shillings he gave me, as I threw it with the others, but I gave them all to the policeman—it was about the same brightness as the others.
Cross-examined. Q. Is there a bell to your shop? A. Yes—it rings when people come in, but it goes to the back of the house—I have sometimes a hundred customers in a day—I am not positive that I had ever seen the prisoner before, but I think I have—he was a minute and a half, or more, in the shop—I am quite certain of him—I charged him at Union-hall with passing bad money, and robbery.
PETER O'NEIL ( police-constable M 99.) On the 7th of Jan., about eight o'clock in the morning. I went to Mrs. Williams's shop—she gave me a shilling,
which she marked; she also gave me two more shillings, a half-crown, and sixpence.
MISS WILLIAMS re-examined. This is the money I gave the policeman—I am quite sure there was only two shillings besides what I received from the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. How late do you keep your shop open? A. Till twelve o'clock—I never left it till it was closed—I went to the drawer, about eight next morning, when the policeman came, and saw the shillings there—there was only my father and mother in the house—they had not been in the shop—my brother never goes behind the counter.
COURT. Q. Was there more than three shillings there on Sunday? A. No—anybody could tell the money was bad.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18440205-785]
785. CHARLES ELLIOTT was again indicted for stealing 10 cheroots, value 1 s.; the goods of Mary Ann Horsley.
MARY ANN HORSLBY . I keep a shop in Little Charlotte-street, Black-friars-road. NOT GUILTY .

786. CHARLES ELLIOTT was again indicted for stealing 2 newspapers, value 1 s., the goods of James Richards.
ELIZABETH RICHARDS . I am the wife of James Richards, of Union-street. On Saturday the 6th of Jan., before eight o'clock at night, the prisoner came and asked for the "Illustrated London News," "Bell's Life," and "Parley's Penny Library"—I said, "Bell's Life" was 6 d., and he said be would only take the two newspapers, which came to 1 s.—I gave him the two papers—he laid down a counterfeit shilling—I said that would not do, and he ran away—he had not gone out of the shop when I said it was bad—I spoke to a man at the door, who brought him back—I marked the shilling, and gave it to the policeman—I had showed it to my husband, but do not think I lost sight of it.
Cross-examined by MR. CROUCH. Q. He did not turn back when you objected to the shilling? A. No, he ran out—he must have heard me say it was bad, unless he was deaf—I do not know whether I spoke as low as I do now—I held the shilling in my hand till my husband came in, which was in about two minutes—the prisoner had the newspapers when he was brought back.
PETER O'NEIL ( policeman.) I was on duty in Union-street, on the 6th of Jan., and saw a crowd at Richards's shop—I went in, and Mrs. Richards gave me 1 s.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of counterfeit coin to the Mint. This is a counterfeit shilling.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it a good imitation? A. I should say it is rather inferior—it might impose on a person not well acquainted with money.
NOT GUILTY .

787. JOHANNA SULLIVAN was indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 1 l.; 15 s.; 3 shirts, 15 s.; 1 pair of breeches, 10 s.; 2 shawls, 12 s. 6 d.; 3 handkerchiefs, 2 s.; 1 sheet, 2 s. 6 d.; 1 gown, 2 s. 6 d.; 7 petticoats, 5 s.; 3 pinafores, 1 s.; 2 pairs of stockings, 8 d.; and 1 half-sovereign, and 4 shillings, the property of Timothy Moran.
MARY MORAN . I am the wife of Timothy Moran, of Star-court, St. George the Martyr, Southwark. The prisoner was tipsy—I took her into my house as she said her husband was angry with her—I left her in the house on the 20th of Jan. when I went out—I returned between seven and eight o'clock in the evening—she was gone, I found my husband's box broken open, and missed the articles now produced.
JOHN RUSSELL ( policeman.) I apprehended the prisoner in George-alley on Sunday morning, the 21st of Jan., and found a shawl, shirt, and handkerchief, in the room she was in.
WILLIAM MARTIN ( policeman.) Ifound two shawls, two petticoats, a gown, sheet, and other articles—some in the room the prisoner was in, and some in the next room, in George-alley—the prisoner was intoxicated, and said nothing.
MARY ANN REDMAN . I am the wife of a policeman, and employed at the Bagnigge-wells station, to search the prisoners. On the 21st of Jan. I found on the prisoner two handkerchiefs, 5 s. 1 d., and a pair of stockings, which the prosecutrix claimed—I told the prisoner I had found the money—she said, "What money? I had no money; I suppose it is the money I took out of the box?"—the stockings were on her legs—she said they were her mistress's.
MARY MORGAN re-examined. These are all mine—we lost about 5 l.; worth of property—she was six or seven nights in my house—I have got all my things back but a few shillings.
GUILTY. Aged 27.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Three Months.

789. PATRICK MURPHY was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Walter Cutchy, in the night of the 8th of Jan., at St. George the Martyr, South wark, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 3 coats, value 1 l.; and 1 waistcoat, value 5 s.; his property.
WALTER CUTCHY . I am a hosier and clothier, and live at 67, Blackman-street, Borough. On the night of the 8th of Jan. my house was closed, and all safe—I went to bed at eleven o'clock—I was awoke about two by the policeman—I went with him into the shop, and found one of the fan-lights over the street-door broken—it was safe when I went to bed—I missed three coats, and one waistcoat, which I have not recovered—I found a stick and two waistcoats behind the door—they bad been removed from the place they occupied over-night.
RICHARD JAMES ( policeman.) On Tuesday morning, the 9th, I was on duty, and saw the prisoner standing on another one's shoulders, with his arm through the prosecutor's fan-light—I ran across the road, and as soon as he dropped off the other one's shoulders, I took him into custody, and conveyed him to the station—I then returned to the shop, called Mr. Cutchy up, and on going into the shop he missed three coats and a waistcoat—I saw some broken glass on the table, and a stick and two waistcoats, which had been knocked down—the goods were hanging upon brass rods, inside the fan-light—I dare say this stick would bring the goods off the rod to the window-there is no hook to it, or anything peculiar about it.
Prisoner. He cannot swear I broke the window; I was coming home; two youg men were passing; they caught my cap away, and chucked it up to he fan-light; I asked them to lift me up, which they did to get my cap; the
policeman was drunk, and used very bad language. Witness. I did not—I found no cap inside the window—I was not drnnk—his cap was on when I took him—the prosecutor's house is in the parish of St. George the Martyr.
JOHN BARTLETT . I work at Mr. Davis's—I saw the constable take the prisoner into custody, and saw his arm through the fan-light over the door.
Prisoners Defence. I got on the man's shoulders to get my cap; no property was found on me.
GUILTY . Aged 19— Transported for Ten Years.

790. WILLIAM SWAINE was indicted , on the 12th of Jan., for stealing 1 ass, price 12 s., the property of Henry Boughton.
THOMAS BENT ( police-constable N 95.) On the 12th of Jan. I saw the prisoner on Clapham-common—he went up to three donkeys, drove them a little way across the common, drove two back, then got a stick and drove the other on alone about a quarter of a mile—he saw another policeman in uniform on the other side of the common, and then turned the donkey into the furze—Iwatched him for a quarter of an hour hiding himself behind the trees—I then asked what he was doing with the donkey—he said Harry Bowden had lost it, and asked him if he found it to drive it there—I asked where he brought it from—he said from Clapham-common—I asked him to show me the donkey—he took me into the furze and showed me another—I said that was not it—I showed him the right one—he said, "Harry Bowden told me to drive it there."
Prisoner. I saw the donkey in Mr. Cubit's park; I knew it would be impounded, and knowing the owner I drove it into the furze, and was going to drive it home.
HENRY BOUGHTON . I am a labourer, and live in Acre-lane, Clapham—I kept an ass at Mr. Parson's, in Acre-lane, near Clapham-common—I had not desired the prisoner to drive it—they are liable to be impounded if astray—if they get into Mr. Cubit's park they are always impounded—that is in Clap-ham-park—I know the prisoner, and should not object to bis keeping it from going there—I had seen it about an hour before.
THOMAS BENT re-examined. It was nearly a mile from Mr. Cubit's park when he first drove it—he was driving it away from home—it was feeding on the road, not going towards Mr. Cubit's park.
NOT GUILTY .

I saw the female prisoner go to Mr. Brooks, the butcher's shop in Britannia-place, Wandsworth-road, and wait at the door two or three minutes—the male prisoner, who I believe is her son, gave her something, which she put under her cloak, and went away—I followed, and asked what she had got—she said, "Nothing"—I said, "I know you have"—she then said it was a breast of mutton she had bought of Mr. Brooks—I saw there were two, and took her back—she afterwards said, "For God's sake let me go, I will tell you the truth; I have an afflicted husband at home, and my son has given it to me"

[trialAccount t18440205-792]
792. CHARLES EDMONDS was indicted for feloniously assaulting Matilda Potter, and attempting to discharge at her a pistol loaded with gunpowder and a leaden bullet, with intent to murder her.—2nd COUNT, stating his intent to be to do her some grievous bodily harm.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
MATILDA POTTER . I am a widow, and reside with my mother, in Bridgehouse-fplace, Southwark—she keeps a coffee-shop—the prisoner lodged at her house, but ceased to do so on the 7th of Oct.—during the latter part of that time he paid his addresses to me, and I saw him at the house from time to time since he left—I was examined at the Police-court on the 16th of Jan.—about six weeks before that he was at the house—I told him my friends did not approve of his visits, and I did not wish him to come any more—he said if he did not have me, no one else should—I have told him since that his visits were not approved of—on Sunday night, the 14th of Jan., I saw him at my mother's house, and told him he must not come again, I should not speak to him if he did—he wished me good bye, and said he should not come any more—on the Monday morning the shop was opened about six o'clock—he came in, and said he had come to see me again and bid me good bye, he should not come again, I should see his corpse before night—my mother came into the room directly after, and said if he did not leave she would charge a policeman with him—he then went away—at five that evening he came through the coffee-room into the bar, and said, "I am come to see you again"—I said, "I see you have"—he had both hands in his coat-pockets—my mother was in the room—he repeated the same words to her, "I am come to see you again"—he then began to abuse her—she told him not to make a disturbance he would disturb the gentlemen in the coffee-room—I said, "Charles, why do you act in such a manner?"—I did not notice any reply—I was standing up, in the act of drawing some coffee, which was on the stove, by the side of the fire, and heard the snap of a pistol which he presented at my left ear—I was standing sideways to him—I heard the snap, turned my head, and saw the pistol within two or three inches of my ear—I felt a pain in my ear for two hours after, from the sound of the snap—my mother flew up to him directly, and pulled his arms back—Davey, who was in the coffee-room, came in and took the pistol out of his hand.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. How long had he been paying attention to you? A. Four or five months—he took a ring off my finger on the 14th of Jan.—I do not suppose he wore it, for he brought it back next morning—my mother and sister were in the room when this occurred—he had to come through the coffee-room to come to where I was—there were more than, half a dozen people in the coffee-room—I have heard him talk about dying for me—he said he had an affection for me—I do not know that it was in strong terms—on the Friday morning before this, I think I gave the prisoner a new shilling to give to my child—I did not tell him it would be the means of ingratiating himself with my mother—he had promised her something, and I gave it him to give her—these two letters are my handwriting, I think—I have not expressed a strong attachment for him—I told him I must see a great alteration in him—he had never before said I should see his corpse before night—he had only one pistol, as far as I know.
SARAH WILLET . I am Mrs. Potter's mother. I did not know the prisoner till he came to my house to lodge on the 27th of May—he occupied a bed-room on the third floor, furnished—while there, I perceived he was making advances to my daughter—I did not approve of it, and gave him notice to leave—he left on the 7th of Oct.—after he left he occasionally came to the
coffee-room to take his meals, but very seldom—I told him I disapproved of it—on Monday morning, the 15th of Jan., I saw him there, and ordered him out of the bar—he bid me good bye, and said I should not see him any more, or something to that effect, and left—he came again about five o'clock in the after-noon—he had a great-coat on, and both hands in the pockets—when he came in at the door he came up to me, and said he was come—I said, "Charles, don't make any noise, you will disturb the gentlemen in the coffee-room"—he was then in the bar, which is a small room we have for the family—he had come through the coffee-room into the bar—I told him I would charge the police with him if he made a noise—on that he turned from me—I do not know what he said, but he then turned from me to my daughter—I do not recollect that he said anything at that moment—he bid me shake hands with him, then turned to my daughter, and said to her, "Here I am again"—she said, "Don't make a noise"—I was sitting on a chair—I lifted my head up in a moment, and saw him holding the pistol to her left ear—I jumped up, and pulled him from her—I did not hear any noise from the pistol, for my daughter Louisa screamed loudly, "Pistol!" and ran into the coffee-room—I cannot tell whether she screamed before or after I lifted up my head, I was so frightened—I ran up to him, and seized both his arms, pushed him to the bar-door to get him into the coffee-room, and Davey, who was in the coffee-room, came up and took the pistol from him—there were several more in the coffee-room—when I pulled him from my daughter he had the pistol to her ear—he looked over his shoulder, and said he would serve me the same—the people in the coffee-room secured him till the policeman came, which was in about a quarter of an hour.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you ever before told any body he said he would serve you the same? A. Yes, several—I mentioned it before the Magistrate—my daughter is a widow, and has not much to give away—I do not think she ever gave the prisoner a shilling.
LOUISA WILLET . I am the daughter of the last witness—I was at home on the 15th of Jan.—the prisoner came in the morning, and again in the afternoon—I saw him come in—he went up to my mother, and said, "I am come to see you again"—he had a great-coat on, and his hands in his pockets—he then went up to my sister, and said the same—she said, "Charles, don't make a disturbance here"—I then saw him present a pistol at my sister's ear—I did not see where he took it from—it was not the handle that was at her ear, it was the other end—I screamed out "Pistol!" and ran into the coffee-room.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you remember his once pretending he had taken laudanum? A. No—I recollect about two months before, mother saying something of their being afraid he had taken some poison—I have seen my sister mending his clothes now and then—I and my mother have called her a great fool for doing so.
ROBERT DAVEY . I am a printer, and work a few doors from Mrs. Willet's, and take my tea there—I was there at tea about five o'clock on the 15th of Jan. with Swift—the prisoner came in, and passed into the back room—I shortly after heard a scuffle—Louisa came running into the coffee-room before that, and said he had got a pistol in his hand—I looked up, and saw him and Mrs. Willet behind him—I saw the pistol in his hand—he was attempting to raise the hammer up again, which was down—there was a cap on the nipple—I took the pistol from him, raised the hammer up, and took the cap off—a constable came in—I gave him the pistol—the cap was split a little at the side—it had not exploded—I think the nipple was too large—the cap was perfect, and the detonating powder in it—I handed the cap and pistol to
the police—I stood guard over the prisoner with others till the policeman came—I asked him if the pistol was loaded—he said, "Yes, with powder and bull," and said he intended to have shot her and himself too—he called Mrs. Willet a d—d old b—h—they were speaking together when I went up to them—I do not exactly recollect his saying any thing else—I saw the pistol in the Magistrate's hands, at Union-hall—he examined it, and a little dust and powder came from the end of the barrel when he unscrewed it—it had then been drawn.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you notice the touch-hole? A. Not till I was at Union-hall—I did not notice that it was clogged up—the Magistrate asked the inspector whether it was clogged or not, and looked at it.
Q. Are you sweet on this widow? A. That is my business.
MR. CLARKESON. Q. Have you paid your addresses to her? A. No.
JOSEPH CLARKE ( policeman.) I was at Mrs. Willet's house on Monday afternoon, the 15th of Jan., and received the prisoner in charge—I produce the pistol and cap which I received from Davey at the house—I asked the prisoner if it was loaded—he said, "Yes, with powder and bail"—the inspector examined it at the station in my presence—the ball laid on the powder—the inspector took it off—here it is, and the powder.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not the touch-hole clogged up? A. It was foul—it would not act—he did not tell me he bad wetted the powder to prevent it acting.
WILLIAM THOMAS TOPP . I am inspector of the L division of Police. I was on duty at the station in Tower-street, Westminster-road, on Monday evening, the 15th of Jan., I examined the pistol produced by Clarke—I unscrewed the barrel, took out a bullet and powder—I am acquainted with the use of pistols—there was a full charge of powder in it—the powder was fine and dry—I emptied it on the hob—I afterwards swept it into the fire with a pen and it exploded—I was present when the prisoner was before the Magistrate—after the witnesses had been examined he was committed for trial—as he was going away Mrs. Potter was there—he used threatening expressions to her—I cannot give his exact words—it was that something should ring in her ears here-after—he would give her something that would ring or tingle in her ears hereafter.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you examine the nipple of the pistol? A. Yes, it appeared to be foul and clogged up.
MR. PAYNE. Q. To what extent was it clogged? A. To no extent, the surface of the nipple was clogged up with some iron-rust as it appeared—it was not in the same state as it is now.
JURY. Q. Did you observe any thing when you swept the powder into the fire? A. The usual explosion.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Transported for Fifteen Year
Before Mr. Justice Coleridge.
[trialAccount t18440205-793]
793. GEORGE JANES and WILLIAM SMITH were indicted for stealing 1 gelding, value 2 l.; 10 s., and 1 mare, 2 l.; 10 s., the property of William Pershouse.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM HARRIS . I am inspector of places for slaughtering horses for St. George the Martyr, Southwark. On the 2nd of Jan., Mr. Winkley, a licensed horse slaughterer, sent for me to take two horses—I went to his premises about half-past eight in the morning, and saw a mare and gelding, which I have since ordered to be given up to Mr. Pershouse's man—the prisoners were there—I asked whose horses they were—Janes said they were his—I asked why he brought them there to be slaughtered—he said one of
them, the bay mare, would not work, and the gelding, he bad changed for a donkey and given 16 s. for it at Mitcham-common, on Saturday last, (this was Tuesday)—he said he lived at Mitcham-common—I asked what part of the common—he said, "Grove-place"—I asked how long he had had the mare—he said, "Ever since Barnet fair"—I asked Smith what he had to do with it—he said he met him at Brixton-hill, and lent him a hand to bring the horses there, that was all he knew of it—I asked Janes what this young man had to do with it—he said, "He only assisted me to bring them from Brixton, that is where I met him,"and Smith said he met him at Brixton-hill—I heard Mr. Winkley tell Janes to go to the counting-house and receive his money—I thought it was all right, and they both went away together—I asked them before they left the yard, "Are you now going back to Mitcham?"—Janes said, "Yes"—they stood alongside each other at the time—they went away together—I followed them full half a mile to see if they went in the road for Mitcham—they then went down Poplar-row in a direction for the Kent-road—I followed and stopped them—I asked Janes where he was going, did he call that the road to Mitcham—he said, "I am going to London-bridge to buy this young man a pair of shoes"—I said it was not the road for London-bridge, he had told me a story, I must take him back and be satisfied he had come by the horses honestly—I took them back to Winkley's yard and told them they must go with me to Mitcham—I told Winkley not to slaughter the horses—I took the prisoners in a cab to Mitcham to see if their statement was true—Janes had said he had chopped the gelding for a donkey and 16 s. with a man who lived on Mitcham-common—I took them to the place pointed out by Janes—I found the man did not live there, nor did he himself live where he said—I brought them back and took them to the station.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did all the conversation about the horses take place in the yard? A. Yes—I began it by asking why they brought them there—Winkley stood very near, and heard the best partof the conversation—the prisoners seemed willing to go to Mitcham.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Was not all Smith said, that he met him and helped him? A. Yes.
MR. HORRY. Q. Did the horses look fit for slaughter? A. Yes—they were worn out, and one had the mange.
WILLIAM PERSHOUSE . I live with my father, William Pershouse, a farmer, at Greenwich. He had the mare and gelding turned out on the Charlton marshes all the summer—I saw them there three days or a week before they were taken away—the two were worth 5 l.;
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Does your father own the vans between Greenwich and Woolwich? A. Yes—he has not disposed of his stock, I swear these two horses are his—I saw them in the marshes night and day—they belonged to the farm—I know them by being both blind in the right eye—the gelding knuckles over the hind joint, which had been sprained—I do not think either of them had the mange—I will not swear it, not having been close to them—they were in rather low condition—they might look fit for the knackers, but answered our purpose.
MR. HORRY. Q. Have you seen the mare and gelding? A. Yes, the day before yesterday, in the upper field, at Charlton.
SAMUEL WEBB . I am a labourer, in the service of William Pershouse, of Greenwich. On the 3rd of Jan. I was at Winkley's, and saw a mare and gelding—they both belong to my master—I saw Harris there—he afterwards gave me the mare and gelding to take home—on the 1st Jan. I had seen them both on the marshes, about half-past three o'clock in the afternoon, in the next marsh to the Anchor and Hope public-house—I missed them about half-past seven
o'clock on Tuesday morning—I found them at Winkley's on Wednesday, the 3rd of Jan.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE? Q. What do you know them by? A. I should know them very well if I saw them a quarter of a mile off by the marks—I have worked with the gelding four years, and have known the other about six months—I am quite sure they are master's—they were on their last legs—Harris, the police-sergeant, was at Winkley's when I taw them.
WILLIAM TOWN . I keep the Anchor and Hope, at Lewisham. I know the prisoners—I saw them on Monday night, the 1st of Jan., at my house, at late as ten o'clock—my house is not near the marshes—Janes hat lived about 150 yards from me, for about two years and a half—he has a small stable there.
Cross-examined. Q. Has he borne an honest character? A. I never knew anything against him.
JAMES WEBB . I live with my father, at Charlton. I know Smith, by going with Pershouse's wagon—on Monday, the 1st of Jan., about three o'clock, or a little after, I saw Smith walking with somebody towards the marshes—they passed through Pershouse's field—Smith hove a snow-ball at my brother—I am quite sure Janes it the one who waa with Smith—I saw him again before the Magistrate.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not you say at first, you did not know Janet? A. Not before that day—he was about five minutes in my sight—I was at work in the field, about half a mile from Charlton marshes—I did not tee the horses till about half an hour after.
JOHN RANDLE ( policeman.) I received the prisoners in charge from Harris on the 2nd of Jan.—I found two sovereigns on Janes.
SAMUEL WEBB re-examined. The two marshes are twelve or fourteen acres—master's cattle run there—the marshes communicate—they might run from one to the other—we only had three horses there at that time.
(Janes received a good character.)
JANES — GUILTY . Aged 26.
SMITH — GUILTY . Aged 25.
confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

793. JOHN DAVIS was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Samuel Brown, on the 10th of Jan., at Christchurch, and stealing therein, 2 knives, value 1 s.; and 1 brush, value 1 s., his property; and that he bad been twice before convicted of felony.
SAMUEL BROWN . I am a chimney-sweeper, and live in Swan-court in the parish of Christchurch. It is my dwelling-house—on Wednesday evening, the 10th of Jan., I left my house about seven o'clock—I shut the door fast, and doubled locked it—I came back about twenty-five minutes past seven, and saw a light in the house, and the door standing slightly open—I saw the prisoner in the house—I watched, and saw him searching the things over in the cupboard—he took up two knives and a brush from the fire-place—he had a large candle alight in his hand—I caught hold of the door, shut it to, and shut him in—I made an alarm, and in half-a-minute there was a score of people round the door—I heard a smash as if the candlestick was thrown into the cupboard, and heard a run up-stairs—in four or five minutes an officer came, who went in, and took him—I am sure he is the man I had seen at the cupboard.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Are you quite certain you double-locked the door? A. Yes—my wife was out—I never saw the prisoner before
to my knowledge—a good many persons live in and near Swan-court—Ihave not lost anything.
ABRAHAM HAND . Iassisted in taking the prisoner into custody—Ikicked against a bag next door to the prosecutor's, which contained eight skeleton keys and a crowbar, which Igave to the officer.
Cross-examined. Q. Whereabout did you find this bag? A. Underneath the prosecutor's window, outside the door.
--------( policeman.) I produce eight keys and a crowbar, which Ireceived from Hand—Ihave tried them to the prosecutor's door, and one of them opens it with the greatest ease.
RICHARD BRANTON ( policeman.) Iproduce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read)—he is the person.
JOHN COLE ( City policeman.) Iproduce another certificate of the prisoner's conviction—( read)—he is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Ten Years.
