794. GEORGE GARDINER was indicted for stealing 1 box, value 2 s. 6 d.; 8 sovereigns; 1 half-sovereign; 24 half-crowns; 110 shillings; and 60 sixpences, the property of Martin Ohler in his dwelling-house.
MARGARET OHLER . Iam the wife of Martin Ohler, a baker. On the 11th of Nov. I left our cash-box standing on the chimney-piece in my sitting-room—it contained about 20 l.;—there were eight sovereigns, a half-sovereign, about twenty half-crowns, some shillings, and sixpences in it—Iam sure it was safe about five o'clock, when Iwent into the shop—while Iwas engaged, the prisoner walked in through the shop, through the sitting-room, to the bake-house—his father was in our employ—Iobserved nothing in his hand when he went in, but when he came out Isaw that he had something in his hand behind him—he looked me sharp in the face, and kept his hand behind him under his coat—thinking it was a loaf, and knowing he was a poor fellow, a long time out of work, Isaid nothing—about half-a-minute after he was gone Imissed the cash-box—no one was there but the prisoner and two lodgers, who came down through the sitting-room, from the time of seeing the cash-box safe, and missing it—it has not been found.
Prisoner. Ihad nothing in my hand—Itook in some yeast—the lodgers came through to shake their carpets at the same time.
MARY ANN TAPLIN . Ilodge in the prosecutor's house. On the 11th of Nov., about five o'clock, Ipassed through the sitting-room—Isaw the prosecutrix in the shop, and saw a man standing by the fire-place in the sitting-room in a stooping position—Icould not swear it was the prisoner, as Idid not look pointedly at him—neither Ior my sister touched the box—we did not see it—we did not stay in the room—we only passed through to shake a carpet.
LUCY TAPLIN . Ilodge in the prosecutor's house—Ipassed through the sitting-room with my sister on this evening—Idid not touch the box—Isaw the prisoner standing in the room close by the fire-place in a stooping position—we went through the bakehouse into the yard, returned in about five minutes, and, as Iwas going up-stairs, the loss of the box was discovered—no one had been there that Iknow of besides the prisoner.
PHCEBE WRIOHT . Ilive opposite the prosecutor's. On the 11th of Nov., about five o'clock, Iwas at my bed-room window, and saw the prisoner come out of Mr. Ohler's shop with something under his arm wrapped in something—it was about the size of half a quartern loaf—Icannot say what it was—Isaw his features—he stopped to speak to his father who was at the barrow and went on—he had on the cap now produced—Icannot speak to any other part of his dress.
THOMAS BLEWITT , clothes salesman. On Saturday, the 11th of Nov., between
six and seven o'clock, the prisoner came to my shop and purchased a toit of clothes and a hat, which came to 2 l.; 5 s.—he paid me all in silver—he had on this drab plush cap, which he left with me, saying that he would call for it again, that he was in a great hurry to go off by the mail train that night—he asked me where he could buy some cheap boots—Irecommended him next door—he also wanted to buy some shirts, and handkerchiefs, and I recommended him to another shop.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF ( police-constable L 83.)Itook the prisoner into custody—Isaid, "Your name is George Gardiner"—he said, "My name is John"—Itold him Iwanted him for stealing a cash-box containing 27 l.;
MRS. OHLER re-examined. The cash-box was about the size of a half quartern loaf—he did not buy any loaf—it was his father who was with the barrow—the father had been in our employ about five months, and he used to go out with his father with the bread—he served several customers, took the money and kept it—Ithink he was in poor circumstances on the 11th. Nov. he had had nothing to do for a long time before.
Prisoner's Defence. The money Ihad Iwon at skittles; Ihave no knowledge whatever of the box; the lodgers had to go through the place as well as I had; I had to go away for a day or two, and when Icame back they wanted me to account for this box, and no one saw me take it.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined One Year.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

796. THOMAS SPARKES was indicted for stealing, at Lambeth, 2 bags, value 2 d.; 11 sovereigns, 20 half-sovereigns, 48 half-crowns, 200 shillings, 120 sixpences, and 6 groats, the property of William John Bayfield, in his dwelling-house.
BENJAMIN LOCKWOOD . Iam in the employ of William John Bayfield, a pawnbroker, No. 73, New Cut, in the parish of St. Mary, Lambeth. The prisoner was in Mr. Bayfield's employ, and left on the 14th of Oct. last—he was in the habit of calling occasionally—on the 22nd of Jan., in the evening Isaw him outside the shop, and went to him—he said, "Is the governor (meaning my master) in the shop?"—Isaid, "No, but be is going out directly and so am I"—Ithen left him, and went in doors—Icounted a quantity of money which amounted to 26 l.; 1 s. 8 d. in silver, 2 l.; in copper, and 21 l.; 10 s. in gold—Itook the silver and gold up stairs—6 l.; 1 s. 8 d. in loose silver Iput into a cash-box—Ialso put 21 l.; 10 s. in gold into the cash-box, and put it into a bureau in my master's bed-room—there was then 20 l.; in silver in two 10 l.; bags which Iput in the bureau by the side of the cash-box—Ilocked the cash-box and bureau, put the keys in a drawer in the same room, and then went out—Ihad lost sight of the prisoner all this time—it was about nine o'clock—when Igot a few doors from the house Isaw the prisoner walk into the house—Iwas in the habit of counting the money, and taking it up stairs every night—the prisoner knew that was the practice—he had lived with us eighteen months—Ireturned home about a quarter to ten, and from something which was said to me by the housekeeper about the prisoner having been a long while in the garden, Iwent to see where he was—I
heard the yard door open distinctly, and said to her," Why here he comes"—I went towards the yard door—I found he was not there, and called to him "Tom, are you coming?"—he said, "Yes, directly"—I then went up stairs to the shop, came down again in a few minutes, and found him sitting down having supper in the kitchen with the housekeeper—I had not sat down more than five minutes when he very hurriedly got up, bid us good night very hastily, and I noticed that he seemed to back out of the kitchen, with his face towards me all the while—I saw no more of him—next morning I went up as usual about eight o'clock to get the money—master was out—I found the keys in the drawer where I had deposited them the night before—I unlocked the bureau and missed the two 10 l.; bags of silver—I unlocked the cash-box, and found 21 l.; in gold gone—I immediately gave information to my employer, told him my suspicions fell on the prisoner, and he gave information to the police—the prisoner, when in our employ, may have taken up the money and put it away when I was out, but he knew perfectly well where the money was kept, and where the keys were deposited.
MARY NICHOLSON . I am in Mr. Bayfield's service as housekeeper, and have been for thirteen years. I know the prisoner—he was in the habit of calling since he left—on the 22nd of Jan., between nine and ten o'clock, when my master and Lockwood were out, he came down to me in the kitchen and directed a letter for me—he then got up, and went into the yard, as I suppose—Lockwood returned about a quarter of an hour after—he called him, and he came back into the kitchen—he sat down to supper near the door—I asked him to come near the fire—he said, "I had much rather sit herel'—as he sat at supper I noticed that his clothes had like dust and flue on them—next day I looked under my master's bed—the dust and flue collects under the bed, and there was an appearance of its being removed from a space, and a paper fire ornament in a large sheet of newspaper which had been under the bed was removed to another part, as if done in getting under the bed—the keys of the cash-box and bureau were kept in the top drawer in Mr. Bayfield's room.
SARAH DEAN . I am servant at the Crown, at Croydon. On the 23rd of Jan., at half-past three o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner arrived there in a horse and chaise, which he had put up, came in, and had some bread and cheese—he went out with the horse and chaise, and returned at half-past eight in the evening with his face very much bruised—he slept at the Crown, and next morning after breakfast he had his bill—I saw him lay a handful of sovereigns on the mantel-piece, about twenty—he had a purse with money, which appeared like silver—he left on foot, and said he should return that evening or early next morning, but did not—the policeman Goff fetched the horse and chaise—his face was not at all scratched when at sapper that night.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF ( policeman.) On Monday, the 28th of Jan., I apprehended the prisoner at Folkestone—I told him it was for stealing 41 l.; from Mr. Bayfield, in the New-cut, on Monday night last—he said, "Bad company has caused this"—I took him into a public-house, searched him, and found on him 6 l.; in gold, 12 s. 6 d. in silver, 5 1/2 d. in copper, a purse, a silver watch and guard, two rings, a union pin, four duplicates, and a cane—as I took him back to town he said he had left a deposit at Mr. Ball's of 10 l.;, and he sent a note to Mr. Ball to fetch the chaise home, as he had broken it—I found the chaise broken—he said he had left the horse at the Crown, at Croydon—I found Ball's chaise at Newington-causeway, and the other at Croydon—there were three broken.
Prisoner's Defence. I am innocent of the charge; when Lockwood called me he found me where I was supposed to have gone.
BENJAMIN LOCK WOOD re-examined. I went into the yard—Idid not lee him, but Iheard him in the water-closet—Ihad been oat about three-quarters of an hour—Icalled to him, and he answered me—Iwent close to the door, and am confident he was there then—when Isaw him enter the house he would have to go through the shop, which leads to the staircase—he could go up stairs or down—he must go down to go into the yard—he could go up to the room where the money was, and when he left Nicholson he could pass the yard door and go up, without going into the shop—when he left us he went into the employ of a pawnbroker, in Cable-street, Rosemary-lane, but he had left there at this time.
JURY. Q. Do you know his circumstances before this? A. Yes—Iknow he had not the means of having 1 l.;, for be told me he had been on a trial here, and got a guinea, and that was almost gone—he said on Christmas eye that if Ihad a job he should be glad to earn 1 s., and Iknow since that he had not been earning 1 s.
MARY NICHOLSON re-examined. He was half an hour in the house before Lockwood came in, and then he was five minutes before be came into the kitchen—he was absent from the kitchen twenty minutes—Idid not observe any flue upon him when he was first in the kitchen, but there was when he came back—when he left he used always to shake hands with us—he did not that night—Iput my band half across to him, but he backed out of the kitchen with his face towards us—when he lived with us he was always near the fire, and this night he sat close to the door, a distance from the fire.
GUILTY .— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18440205-797]
797. THOMAS SPARKES was again indicted for stealing, on the 8th of Oct., 1 watch, value 3 l.;, and 1 guard-chain, value 10 s.; the property of William John Bayfield, his master.
BENJAMIN LOCKWOOD . On the 8th of Oct., the prisoner was in Mr. Bayfield's employ—two lads were also in the service—we had a pledge made on the 1st of Dec., 1842, of a watch and guard, in the name of William Pinkney, of Grove-place—that watch was afterwards applied for and missed—the prisoner left on the 14th of Oct.
JOHN JAMES PINKNEY , general dealer, Grove-place, Lambeth. This watch and guard is mine—Ipledged it at Mr. Bayfield's on the 1st of Dec., 1842, in the name of Pinkney, of Grove-place, for 1 l.; 6 s.,—Iapplied to take it oat at the latter end of November last year—Iam quite sure this is the watch.
Prisoner. Q. How do you know it? A. By the number 20, 229 and the maker's name, Duchesne.
MARY CHRISTINA GOODWIN . Iknow the prisoner—Ipledged a watch at his desire at Mr. Jones's for 1 l.;, on the 8th of Oct. in the name of Clyne, and on the 11th got 10 s. more on it—Isent the ticket to the prisoner in sletter—this watch is a great deal like the one, and Ibelieve it to be the same.
Prisoner. Q. Did you pledge it at my request? A. Yes, you gave it to me—Icannot say that Iever saw you with it before Ipledged it—Icannot wear this is the watch—is is a great deal like it.
Charles Cripps. Iam in the service pf Thomas Jones, a pawnbroker, in St. George's-circus. On the 9th of Oct. a watch without a glass, and a guard, were pledged in the name of Clyne—it was afterwards redeemed by the prisoner—he employed me to get a glass to it—he pledged it again himself—it was again redeemed on the morning of the 23rd of Jan. but not by him—some other property of his was redeemed at the same time to the amount of 3 l.; 5 s., and was paid for all in silver.
Prisoner. Q. Was that watch ever pledged at your shop before Goodwin pledged it? A. No, Iam sure of that.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF ( police-constable L 83.) I apprehended the prisoner at Folkestone, and found this watch on him.
Prisoner's Defence. I expected to have witnesses to prove I had the watch a long time prior to entering Mr. Bay field's service; Pinkn ey has been shown the watch before, so as to know the number.
JOHN JAS. PINKNEY re-examined. I had not noticed the number before I lost it, but I went to Mr. Carter, the chronometer maker, on Cornhill, who cleaned it for me three years ago, and had it in his hands two or three times, and he gave me the number from his book—they always take the numbers of the watches they clean—I have had it seven years, and am quite positive it is mine—I can recognize it from the dial, and from having it in use so many years, independent of the number.
GUILTY . Aged 20.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
Before Mr. Justice Cresswell.
[trialAccount t18440205-798]
798. GEORGE GIFFORD was indicted for feloniously assaulting Henry Howard, and stabbing and wounding him on the left side of the chest, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY HOWARD . corn-porter, Dale's-place, Stoney-lane, Tooley-street. On Thursday night, the 18th of Jan., between twelve and one o'clock, I had been taking a drop of porter, but was not the worse for liquor—I met a female, and went with her to a house in Vine-yard, Tooley-street, kept, I believe, by the prisoner—we went into the top room—I took up the poker, joking-like, and swung it round in my hand—I was talking to the female when the prisoner came up, and asked me for the money for the room—I refused to give it him just then, and said I would send it down by the young woman in a minute or two (I had not been in the room three minutes when the prisoner came in) he asked for the money again, and I said again that I would send it down—I had the poker in my hand, and was swinging it round, and singing a bit of a song, "The Sprig of Shillalah and Shamrock so Green"—the prisoner then said, "Oh, is that what you mean?" and left the room, shutting the door after him—he came up again with a drawn sword, and said, "Now, you go out of my house"—he made a cut over my head—I held up my arm, and happened to catch the flat part of the sword—he then stabbed me in the breast—I was so irritated I did not exactly see how it was done—I had on this coat and waistcoat—my breast was cut, and bled very much—the poker was at that time in the fender—I put it there while he had gone down for the sword—I did not think he was going to fetch it, or I would not have put it there—I had nothing in my hand at the time I was stabbed—I instantly ran down stairs, and found a policeman—I was taken to Guy's hospital, and had my wound dressed—I remained there till the Monday morning—I was hardly able to move a limb—I then went to Union-hall—I was not able to leave the hospital sooner—I had pains all over my body, limbs and back, and it made me bad all over—I could hardly fetch my breath—I saw the wound—it was a kind of stab—it struck right against my breast bone I expect—I had on this coat—I had made no attempt to strike the prisoner before he made this blow at me.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Where did you meet with the female? A. At the Waterman's Arms, about eleven o'clock—her name is Simpson—we did not drink together—I had been drinking previously with some friends from nine o'clock—I drank porter—I was not drunk—I had
been to the prisoner's house before—I did not brandish the poker round my head, I only twisted it round in my hand, holding it in the middle—I do not know whether that is the way they fight with shillalahs—the prisoner came in while I had it in my hand—I do not know what "Sprig of shiilalah" means—I believe the prisoner said I must either pay him then or go away—I did not then take up the poker, and brandish it—I said, "You can wait a minute or two surely "—I began to brandish it as he was about leaving the room—I did not say, "Show me the man that will turn me out; I will give you a taste of sweet Erin go bragh"—when the prisoner brought the sword up, he said he would give me Erin go bragh—I did not use that expression—I did not in flourishing the poker very nearly strike the girl—I was a distance off both of them—I put down the poker while he was gone for for sword, and never took it up again—I had not the poker in my hand at the time he came in with the sword, nor did I strike a violent blow with it at his head, which he received on the sword—I did not then rush towards him—I ran back—I have known Simpson five or six months, and have been with her to the prisoner's house two or three times—I have been in the habit of giving the money for the room to her to take down to pay, and come up again—I do not think I ever flourished the poker on any previous occasion—I never had any altercation with the prisoner before—this room was a double-bedded one, and two persons were in the other bed.
ISABELLA SIMPSON . I am an unfortunate female, and live in Vine-yard, Tooley-street, in the prisoner's house, and have done so five months—on this Thursday night I saw the prosecutor in Vine-yard—he went home with me to the prisoner's house and into the top room—the prisoner came of stairs along with me into the room, and asked the prosecutor if he was going to stop all night—he said, "Yes," and he asked him for the rent of the room—he said, "Do not be so fast," he would send the money down stairs along with me—the prosecutor had a poker in his hand swinging it about, and saying, "Sprig of shillalah and shamrock so green"—he did not make any attempt to strike any body—the prisoner went down stairs, and came up with his sword in about five minutes—he appeared very angry, and said, "Here's for Erin go bragh," rushed forward at the prosecutor, and struck him in the breast with the sword.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the thrust with the sword almost immediately after he said, "Here's for Erin go bragh?" A. Yes; I had not been drinking with the prosecutor—I met him about half an hour before we went to the house—I had seen him in the Waterman's Arms, but did not drink with him at all—I had a little, but not much—at the time the prisoner came up with the sword, the poker was down at the fire-side—the prosecutor put it down immediately the prisoner went down stairs—I was sitting in a chair by the fire when he came up again, and the prosecutor was standing by the fire—he had all his clothes on—there was a male and female in the other bed.
PATRICK M'CORMACK ( police-constable M 59.) On Thursday night, the 18th of Jan., between one and two o'clock, I went to the prisoner's house and asked the prisoner if there had been a disturbance there previously—he said no, every thing had been very quiet—I found this sword in the front parlour at the head of a bed—the prisoner was in the passage—I only searched that room.
THOMAS HICKEY ( police-constable M 152.) I was on duty in Tooley-street on the morning in question, and saw the prosecutor at the top of Vine-yard—he called out "Police"—I went to his assistance—he said he was stabbed—I looked at his breast, and it was all over blood—I directed him to be taken to the hospital, and returned to the prisoner's house—I found him
there, and asked if his name was Day—he said, "No"—I asked if he was the landlord of the house—he said, "Yes"—I said, "You are my prisoner; you have stabbed a man"—he said, "Yes I did"—I asked why—he said he did it in his own defence—I said, "Why, did the man strike you?"—he said, "He was going to strike me with the poker"—I took him into custody.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he say Howard had rushed forward on him with the poker? A. Not at that time; he said so at the station, and that he had the sword to protect himself.
HENRY STANLEY ROBERT PEARSE . I was house-surgeon at Guy's Hospital—about half-past one on Friday morning, the 19th of Jan., I saw Howard, and discovered a punctured incised wound in his left breast—there had been what surgeons would call very inconsiderable bleeding—I saw this sword the same evening—it was such a wound as might have been inflicted with a thrust from this sword through his clothes—he quitted the hospital on the following Monday—I dressed the wound and attended him—the wound of itself was trivial, and there were no after symptoms at all dangerous—I examined bis clothes—they were cut through—the collar of his coat was twice perforated—I probed the wound—it was very slight—I should say it was nearly an inch deep, but the surrounding parts were much swollen, which made it appear deeper than it really was.
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 71.— Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Justice Coleridge.
[trialAccount t18440205-799]
799. HENRY NEWMAN was indicted for feloniously forging an acceptance to a bill of exchange for 20 l.;, with intent to defraud Mark Markwick.—2nd COUNT, for uttering the same.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
MARK MARKWICK . I reside in Hooper-street, Red Lion-square. I have some property in the neighbourhood of Romford, in Essex—I was living at Brixton in 1842—I had occasion to build some houses at Romford and Brentwood—Mr. Edwards introduced me to the prisoner, and he entered into a contract with me for taking some land, and building houses upon it—I advanced him money weekly to pay his men, and on other occasions—in March and April, 1843, he borrowed money of me—he borrowed 13 l.; for a particular purpose—he said there was an innkeeper to whom be owed some money, and I think he had a bill due for 13 l.;, and begged I would let him have 13 l.; to pay it—I gave him a check for 13 l.;—he said Mr. Pain had disappointed him in a loan or payment of money to him, and on the following Wednesday he would bring me the 13 l.;—he never returned that money to me—he continued working for me for about three months—his advances from time to time became much greater than the work he had done, and I found him in great difficulties, so that he could not keep on the spot to look after his men—when I applied to him about the 13 l.;, he said he would get me a bill of 30 l.; of Mr. Pain's, at two months' date—I had never seen Mr. Pain—I had heard of him by name, and believed him to be a respectable man, a surveyor—no particular time was named for the bill—it went on from week to week, I became alarmed, and pressed him to bring me Mr. Pain's bill for 30 l.;—on the 17th of May he brought me this bill at two months for 20 l.;, stating that Mr. Pain refused to give a bill for more than 20 l.;, and for a less period than three months—this is the bill—( bill read)—he told me previously where Mr. Pain lived—when the bill became due, it was presented through my bankers—I never got the money.
HENRY PAIN . I am a surveyor. Last May I was living in Loughborough-road,
North Brixton—the acceptance to this bill it not in my handwriting—I have no recollection of authorising the prisoner to accept this bill, and sign my name to it—I know the prisoner—he has done several jobs for me, and others I have recommended him to.
Q. You have no recollection of having authorised him to sign your name: do you mean, you might have given him authority, and forgot it? A. I mean, of course, that I might have given him authority—from our conversation he might have assumed it.
COURT. Q. You mean, he might have understood you gave him authority? A. He might have done so—we have had several conversations together—he might have thought so.
MR. DOANE. Q. You have had several conversations, do you mean about bills of exchange? A. Not bills of exchange particularly— bond fide matters—I have had several conversations with him about bills of exchange—I have drawn upon him, and he on me—I do not recollect that I ever had any conversation with him about a bill for 30 l.; at two months, or about a bill for 30 l.; at all—I cannot say I had not—we may have had conversation about a 30 l.; bill about May last—I was examined about this matter before the Justice on two occasions—I do not think my memory has at all improved since then with respect to having or not authorised the prisoner.
COURT. Q. You were examined on oath? A. Yet—I have a recollection of what I stated on that occasion.
Q. That being so, do you now mean to say you may have authorised him to use your name by way of acceptance? A. I was called very suddenly to go there, and could not bring all the circumstances directly to my mind—I did not know what I was called for hardly—I do not think I ever gave him authority to use my name in putting an acceptance to this bill—I have no recollection of it—I do not recollect that I evgr did—I never taw the bill till after it was due—I never gave him a direct authority to put my name there without my seeing it—he may have misunderstood me, and thought he had that authority—he might have taken it for granted, but not a direct authority I never could.
Q. Do I understand you think you may have used such language as may have justified him? A. That is my meaning—he might certainly think he was justified in using my name—in his own idea he might think so.
NOT GUILTY
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18440205-800]
800. WILLIAM GARDINER was indicted for feloniously assaulting Thomas Lemon, and cutting and wounding him on the right cheek, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
THOMAS LEMON, carman, Clark's-row, Brixton. On Monday evening, the 5th of Feb., between seven and eight o'clock, I was at Mr. Griffiths's yard, engaged in putting a horse into a stable—the prisoner, who is horse-keeper to Mr. Griffiths, had some words with me about the hone sweating—he told me it was a pretty way to bring a horse home—I said, "I know how to drive a horse, without your directing me"—he directly said he would beat me out of the stable, and beat my head off—I told him he had better do it—I went and hung my whip up, and he took up the bottom of a sconce of a stable lantern, and sent it at me, as I went out of the stable—it caught the side of my cheek and ear—I was blinded with blood almost immediately—I thought my eye was out almost—I told him I would give him into custody—he said he did not care if he had knocked my head off, or brains out, I will not be sure which—I got a policeman, and gave him into custody—I have
been affected by this from that time—I had it dressed—I have suffered weakness from loss of blood, and been in great pain—it is getting easier now—this is the sconce—( produced.)
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. You had overworked the horse? A. It was very slippery—I had not been drinking—I had but one pint of beer all the afternoon—we had not been drinking together—he did not look as if he had been drinking—I had been in Mr. Griffiths's employ, off and on, three or four years, and the prisoner seven or eight—I was not angry with him—I told him if there was anything amiss to speak to Mr. Griffiths about it—I was not saucy—he said a great many aggravating words to me—he repeatedly said he would knock my head off, and I said he might do it if he thought proper—I had said nothing to him before that—I swear I did not offer to do any thing to him—there was a long candle in the sconce just lighted—he was three or four yards from me—after the injury he told me to go to a doctor—he did not say in my hearing that he would pay the expenses, or that he was very sorry that I was hurt—he said he would pay the dressing of it—nothing has been done but putting some plaster on it, and keeping it clean—I have spit blood, and blood has run out of my nose ever since it has been done.
JAMBS SALTER ( police-constable V 15.) I searched the shed at Mr. Griffiths's yard, and found this sconce on the ground, apparently concealed by chaff and dust.
JAMES MURPHY ( police-constable V 261.) I took the prisoner into custody—I told him what for—he resisted very much.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it soon after the complaint? A. About a quarter of an hour.
JOHN PRICE . I keep a chemist's-shop. Isaw the prosecutor's cheek, he had a contused and incised wound, about an inch below the orbit—it was not a bad wound—there appeared to have been a good deal of blood from it—it is not likely to do him any permanent injury.
GUILTY of an Assault only— Confined Three Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

803. WILLIAM HAM MITT was indicted for stealing 36 lbs. weight of rope, value 5 s., the goods of John Clark and another, in a barge on the navigable river Thames, and that he had been before convicted of felony.
GEORGE FREDERICK BENNETT . I am in the service of John Clark and another, at Bankside. This rope ( looking at it) is theirs—I saw the prisoner nnwreatbing it from the rings of two barges which laid at Victoria-wharf, adjoining Southwark-bridge, on the Thames—he coiled it round the barge Robert and walked up the plank with it to the Rose and Bell public-house—I said, "That is our rope"—he said, "It is my own," and walked off with it.
AMELIA COPE . I am the wife of-Charles Cope, of Gravel-lane. On Saturday evening the 27th Jan. the prisoner brought this rope to me for sale, and I bought it of him.
WILLIAM WEED . I am in the service of Messrs. Clark and Wells—I know the prisoner by working on the river—I know this rope is my masters' property—I
missed it from the craft at a quarter before six o'clock on Saturday evening, the 27th Jan.
Prisoner. Q. Can you swear to it? A. Yes, from the whipping of it in two places, where it had been worn.
JAMES WILLIAM GRIFFIN ( Thames police-inspector.) I took the prisoner—I told him what for—he said he had not been there at all that evening—I found the rope at Cope's.
AUSTIN EDWARD GANN ( Thames police-constable.)—I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 43.— Transported for Ten years.

804. EDWARD MAY was indicted for stealing 9 1/2 lbs. weight of cake, value 8 s., the goods of Samuel Frazer Wooller Tomkinson.
SAMUEL FRAZER WOOLLER TOMKINSON . I am a pastrycook, and live in Deptford-row, Newington. On the 8th Jan. I lost part of a cake from a stall at my door—it was brought in to my shop—I knew it by two pins which were stuck into it where the paper had been pinned on.
JOHN ROBERT MOSLEY . 805. ELIZABETH REYNOLDS was indicted for stealing 1 shawl, value 1 l.; 1 pillow, 5 s.; 2 sheets, 15 s.; 1 blanket, 5 s.; 1 tablecloth, 6 d.; and 1 petticoat, 3 s., the goods of John James Bafry.
MARTHA BARRY . I am the wife of John James Barry, of Paradise-buildings, Knightsbridge. The prisoner came to lodge at my house, and left on the 13th of Dec.—I missed a shawl and other things—these are them—I lost fife pocket handkerchiefs and a habit shirt from my private apartment.
JOHN ARCHIBALD , pawnbroker. I produce these things—I took them in of the prisoner on the 15th and 17th of Nov., the 18th, 25th, and 2nd of Dec.—they are all identified by the prosecutrix.
Prisoner's Defence. I was driven to the utmost extremity, having buried three of my children previously; she had lent me one shawl and one petticoat, she sent me to pledge for herself; she also leat me a hand kerchief, and then she threatened to give me in charge. I had two of these things in my possession. I gave up the duplicates, and said I would return them as soon as I could; she threatened me with a policeman, and I left the house with her shawl on.
MARTHA BARRY re-xamined. I did not send her to pledge anything—she said she had been ill, and was very poorly—I waked on her for the first five weeks, and then she said she was much better, and would not allow me to make her bed or do anything.
GUILTY . Aged 42.— confined six months.
[trialAccount t18440205-806]
806. JOSEPH KENT was indicted for stealing 1 stand cock, value 40 s.; 1 stand pipe, 40 s.; 1 branch of a fire engine, 20 s.; 1 branch of an engine, 20 s.; 1 metal pipe, 40 s.; 1 other metal pipe, 40 s.; 15lbs. weight of brass, 7 s.; 5lbs. weight of copper, 4 s.; and 15lbs. Weight of mixed metal, 7 s. the goods of the inhabitants of St. Olave, South wark.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES MEAD . I am beadle and engineer of the parish of St. Olave, South wark—I have charge of the engine—after the fire at St. Olave's it was kept in the arch No. 9, at the bottom of King's Head-yard, Tooley-street—I kept the key of the arch. On the 23rd of Jan. I had my attention called to the archway—the evening before, the carpenter, whom I brought, secured the arch-door with a padlock—the stand-pipe and branch were there safe at eleven o'clock that night—my attention was called to it in the morning by a policeman, saying the place was broken open—I went about half-past seven o'clock—I then missed the stand cock, pipe, and branch—the prisoner keeps a marine-store and rag shop in Bermondsey-street—on Friday, the 26th, I saw the stand cock, and branch, which I missed, in the possession of the policeman who brought it from Mr. Paine's, in the lane—the prisoner's shop is about two or three hundred yards from Tooley-street.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was that all the property which was produced? A. No, here is more—it was entire when I saw it the night before, and was worth between 3 l.; and 4 l.; when new—the prisoner's shop is in a very bad neighbourhood—there are plenty of bad persons about where it was stolen from.
JAMES MILLERY . I am in the employ of Mr. Paine, brass-founder. Shoe-lane. I know the prisoner—between ten and eleven in the morning of the 24th of Jan. I purchased these thirty-nine pieces of metal of him—they would make a stand-cock and stand-branch of a fire-engine if they were entirev—it was broken when I bought it—I gave him 5 d. a pound for the brass, and 8 d. for the copper—I paid him 7 s. 5 d. altogether—there was a short man with him—about half an hour after, I delivered the metal to the officer.
Cross-examined. Q. There was nothing to attract your attention at being suspicious? A. Nothing—I only took about a minute to buy it—I had often bought of the prisoner before—I went with the policeman to his shop on the Thursday.
GEORGE WARDLE ( City police-constable, No. 325.) Itook the prisoner on Thursday, the 25th of Jan., at his own house, in Bermondsey-street—I told him I came respecting some metal he bad sold to Mr. Paine, in Shoe-lane, on the Wednesday—he said, "I have sold some metal to Mr. Paine"—I said I was a policeman, and the metal be sold belonged to a fire-engine—he said he bad sold no such metal as that to Mr. Paine—I said I must take him.
THOMAS BARNES' ( City police-constable, No. 334.) From information I received, I went to Mr. Paine's, on Wednesday, the 24th—I there found this metal, with the name of the parish on—it has been indented, but the name is very distinct—it was by Mr. Paine's request that I looked at it.
RICHARD FULLER ( police-constable M 120.) Iwas on duty at seven o'clock in the morning of the 24th of Jan., and noticed the arch in which the engine was kept—at a quarter past seven I found the archway was broken open, and part of the engine gone—I found the padlock in the mud.
JOHN HIOHWOOD ( police-constable M 102.) Iwas on duty by this archway on the night of the 23rd, and morning of the 24th—the lock was on safe at twenty minutes past six o'clock.
JAMES MEAD re-examined. These are the pieces which, when entire, would form the stand-cock and stand-branch of our engine.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18440205-807]
807. ELIZABETH MARY JONES was indicted for feloniously taking away a certain female child, aged about four months, named Selina Hellens,with intent to deprive Edward Hellens and Frances Hellens, the parents, of the possession of the said child.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be with intent to steal 1 frock, value 5 s.; 1 pinafore, 1 s.; 1 cap, 1 s.; and 1 hood, 4 s.; the goods of the said Edward Hellens, on and about the person of the said child.
MR. WILDB conducted the Prosecution.
FRANCES HOLLENS . I am the wife of Edward Hellens, we live in Wolverly-street, Gloucester-street, Hoxton. On the 27th of Nov. last, I had an infant daughter named Selina—she was four months and a fortnight old within one day—on that day my elder daughter Eliza took the child out at twelve o'clock in the forenoon—she left with the child in her arms as the beerman went past—I had told her not to go out of the street—I did not see the child again till the 3rd of Jan., 1844—I knew it was my child—she was very much reduced—the flannel petticoat which was on her was mine, and was on her on the 27th of Nov.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. Was the child weaned when with you? A. No, and it sucks now—I had fed her just before she went out, and it is now, by care and nourishment, recovering its appearance—it was clean and comfortable when it was returned—this is it.
ELIZA HELLENS . I am going on for eleven years of age. I am the daughter of Frances Hellens, and was living with her—on the 27th of Nov. I went out with my sister Selina—I went into Gloucester-street—a young woman came to me, and said, "Little girl, your bonnet is untied"—she asked me to let her tie it, and she did—she then told me she had a son who was gone for a soldier, and had stopped out all night drinking, and said, "I want such a nice little girl as you to go into a public-house and see if there is a soldier there"—I had my little sister still in my arms—the young woman then took her out of my arms, and told me to go into a public-house and look into the tap-room, and see if there was a soldier there—I went into a public-house—she then sent me into a little cake shop, and gave me a penny piece, and told me to buy a lady's-finger, and I did so—she then told me to go to another shop, and get a pound-cake for the baby—I did so—she told me to ask for a piece of paper—she wrapped up the cake and the lady's-finger, and put them into her pocket—she then sent me into another public-house—she then sat down on a step,. and gave me a fourpenny-piece, and told me to go and buy 1 d. worth of plum-pudding, and to get 1 d. loaf, and she told me the first public-house she came to, she would sit down and have a pint of beer—she held the child while I went on all the errands—she then sent me for 2oz. of cheese, and I went—we got on to the London-hospital, and then she told me she wanted to go up a court for a minute—she went, and took the baby, and I never saw her nor the baby afterwards—she had told me to stop at the end of the court, and I waited a long time—I do not know how long—a woman said I waited twenty minutes—a good many people spoke to me, and a young man took me to the station, told the people I had lost a baby, and then that young man took me home—the woman who spoke to me, and took the baby, was dressed in a half-mourning gown, a black-crape bonnet, and a dark shawl—she had a crape flower by the side of her bonnet—she had a pale complexion, and she had a flat curl on her cheek—I believe her hair was dark—I was examined before the Magistrate, at Union-hall—I did not know the female who was produced to me, at first, but when I came to see her have the bonnet and shawl on, I recognized her—when I saw her first at Union-hall she was dressed very nicely, not in mourning—she was dressed better than when I had seen her before—the constable showed me a crape bonnet, and, to the best of my recollection, that was similar to the
bonnet the woman had on on the 27th of Nov.—I also saw a shawl—the prisoner is the person I saw on the 27th of Nov.
Cross-examined. Q. But the first time you went before the Magistrate, you said she was not the person? A. Yes—I went before the Magistrate three times—I said, both the first and second time, that she was not the person—she had a red face—the third time I went I said she was the person—she had the bonnet and shawl on then—I believe there was a policeman with me when I said she was the person—I said the woman who came up to me, and took the child away, stuttered or stammered in her talk.
MARIA GOODFELLOW . I am the wife of George Goodfellow, of Winchester-court, Monkwell-street. I know the prisoner—I remember seeing her in Oct. last—she told me she was going to have a nurse child—on the 27th of Nov. I saw her at my house, between two and three o'clock in the afternoon—she said she had been to fetch the baby which she was going to have to nurse, and she brought it with her at the same time—I afterwards saw the child at the police-office—I could not swear that it was the same child, but it appeared to me, though very much altered, to be the same—I thought it was the same—I went with the prisoner to Mrs. Cook's.
COURT. Q. How was the prisoner dressed on the 27th, when she brought the child? A. I cannot decidedly say—she was in half-mourning.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you have known her about five years? A. Yes—I do not know whether she was on intimate terms with a sea captain—I was intimate with her, but not particularly in her confidence.
Q. If you had seen that child without having been told whence it came, do you think you should have had any idea that that was the child you had seen before? A. No, Sir.
COURT. Q. Do you think you should have traced the resemblance, so as to believe it to be the child? A. I do not know.
REBECCA COOK . I am the wife of Thomas Cook, of Richardson-street, Bermondsey. The prisoner came to lodge at my house on the 23rd of Oct.—she was in mourning—I asked if she was a widow—she said no, she was a married woman—she went out that evening to see her little boy, as she said—she told me about a Captain Jones, who was then away, but would return the latter end of Dec. or the beginning of Jan—she said he was her husband, but afterwards she said he was not her husband—she told me the had been put to bed on the 12th of April with twins—she was lodging with me in Nov.—I remember her going out about the end of that month—I cannot say exactly how she was dressed, but she was in mourning—I have seen a bonnet produced by the officer—that was left in my room, and it is a similar bonnet to the one she had on when she went out in Nov.—she was going to throw it on the dust-hole on the 30th of Dec, when she left my room, and I begged it of her—on the 27th of Nov. she went out about ten in the morning, and returned between seven and eight in the evening with an infant—she said she had had a few words with a person, and had brought her baby home, and it was a little boy—I thought it was her own—it was called John—I did not find it out to be a girl till the Friday before she left on the Saturday—the captain came on the Friday night, and then the prisoner said the child was to be turned into a girl, and to be called Alice, because the captain's father was to give it 20 l.; and a coral—I said how was that to be done; how was the old gentleman to know whether it was a girl or a boy—she said it was to be kept up as a joke—she gave me a shawl, but I do not know that I ever saw it on her back.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you have an opportunity of ascertaining whether
she had milk in her breasts or not? A. No—the captain appeared very fond of her, and the child was represented to him as his own.
THOMAS BOULTON ( police-constable M 130.) On the 3rd of Jan. I went with another officer and the father of this child to a house where the prisoner lived—I saw her with the child in her arms—the father shook his bead and called her a guilty woman, and then she gave the child over to him—I saw this flannel petticoat taken off the child by its mother, and here is the letter "H." on it—the mother said it was her own making—I got this shawl and crape bonnet from Goodfellow.
DONALD MURRAY ( police-constable M 119.) On the 3rd of Jan. I went with Boulton with a search-warrant to the prisoner's house in George-street, Bermondsey—Mr. Hellens was with me—the prisoner came on the stairs with the child in her arms—Iasked her if Captain Jones was at home—she said, "No"—I asked if she was Mrs. Jones—she made no answer—she handed the child over to Mr. Hellens and said, "I am innocent, but I have bees a mother"—I had a search-warrant to look for the clothes, and the prisoner said, "You will find none; they are all consumed in the flames"—I said, "Not the flannel petticoat and all?"—she said, "No, that is the only article remaining."
Cross-examined. Q. Did she not say, "I have been a mother to it?" A. I did not understand that—she might say so—I understood her to say, "I am innocent, but I have been a mother."
GUILTY. Aged 30.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury, considering the unfortunate situation she stood in at the time as an unfortunate female, and on account of the temptation under which she acted.
Transported for Seven Year
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18440205-808]
808. HENRY KNIGHT was indicted for stealing 180lbs. weight of lead, value 15 s., the goods of John Brighton his master; and JOSEPH HARRA-DIN , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen, &c.; against the Statute, &c.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution,
HENRY PINFOLD . I am in the employ of Mr. Brighton, plumber, Tooley-street. He had some work at a house in George-street. Borough—Knight was employed to assist me in taking up the remainder of the gutters, and finish putting the sink there—on Tuesday week we took home a quantity of lead from the gutters to Mr. Brighton's shop, and left a great deal behind—I bad cut off this piece of lead from the portion I had carried home—I have compared it with the lead produced by the policeman, it corresponds and enables me to say that it is a portion of the lead from the house in George-street—it weighs 112 pounds, and is worth 15 s.—I bad orders from Daley and Inskipper to take the old lead from their factory home to Mr. Brighton, and he was to replace it with zinc
JOHN BRIGHTON . I was employed by Daley and Inskipp to do something to the gutters of their factories—the old lead that was in the gutters was my property.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. How do you make that out? A. Having received instructions to lay down zinc and give them credit for the old lead, and a portion of it was on my premises.
THOMAS WEST ( police-constable M 249.) About a quarter to six, in the evening of Tuesday week I was on duty—I saw Knight in George-street Bermondsey, about a quarter of a mile from the factory, going in a direction from there to Harradin's with two large pieces
of lead on his shoulder—I followed him to Harradin's—it is a marine store shop at Dockhead—Harradin was standing at the door—when Knight came up he went inside—Harradin stopped at the door for a minute and a half, and then followed him in—Harradin was inside for two or three minutes—he came out again, looked up and down the street, and then went in again—after he had been in two or three minutes Kinght came out again—he had nothing with him then—no person went in besides the prisoner before I went to the shop—Knight went back the way he came—I then went into the shop, and asked Harradin what the man had brought who had just gone out—he said he had brought half a cwt. of old iron—I asked him if he brought nothing else—he said, "No"—I asked him to allow me to see it—he asked for a light and showed me some old iron—he then said, "There was a person brought some sheet iron, but I did not buy it"—I said, "A man that has just gone out"—he said, "He brought half a cwt. of old iron, I paid him 2 s. and some halfpence"—I saw some lead in the back parlour which appeared to correspond with that Knight had carried in—I was going into the back parlour—Harradin pushed me back and said he should not allow me to search his place without some authority—I showed him my staff—he said he should not allow me without I had a search warrant—and he said, "Wait till the master comes"—I did not understand what he meant—I said, "If you bought the iron, what do you want with the master"—at that time Clark brought in Knight—I went into the back parlour and began to bring the lead out—Harradin said, "I have not bought the lead"—I took him—as we were going to the station Harradin escaped from me—I threw the lead down, caught him, and turned him back—he became very violent and knocked my head up against the wall—I got the lead again, and produce it—I have seen it matched with that produced by Pinfold, and it corresponds.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not another policeman present a great part of this time in the shop? A. No—a policeman named Turner came up, who was sent in by Clark—Harradin did not tell me that he had not bought the lead—he refused to buy it till the man brought his master to say that it was all right—he told me I was to wait till the master came—that was after I had seen the lead—but he was not aware that I had seen it, because his wife came and covered it while I was talking to him—I said I should put the handcuffs on Harradin—Harradin said, "I will go quietly"—I was present when Knight was brought up—I did not hear him say that he had been sent away by Harradin to fetch the foreman—I did not make Harradin's nose bleed—I will not swear I did not—Wright told me if I would let Harradin alone he would go quietly—that was after he had made his escape, and I had taken him—he ran up to two men, and I took him from them—Clark had gone to the station, and Pinfold was gone on with the biggest part of the lead—it weighed about 801 b.
ROBERT CLARK ( police-constable M 258.) I was with West in George-street—I saw Knight carrying some lead to Harradin's—I took Knight after he had come out, without the lead, about fifty yards from the house—I asked what he went into Harradin's house with—he said, "Nothing"—I asked what he went in for—he said, "—Some money which he left some iron for the night before"—I asked what he paid him—he said, "2 s. 3 d."—Isaid I must go back to the shop—in going back he said he would tell the truth if I would not hurt him, that it was lead he took at the station—Harradin said he had not seen Knight before till that night—I took possession of a piece of lead at Harradin's shop—it matches one of the larger pieces.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you remember anything being said about the foreman? A. His name was mentioned two or three times—I was not in
the shop—I took Knight to the station, and left West, and sent another constable to him—West was not out of temper at all—I did not see Harradin's, nose bleeding—I saw him at the station.
Knight. I went to help Mr. Pinfold, and the gentleman on the premises came up, and said, "Get this lead away this afternoon." Pinfold said to me, "Some of this belongs to us for allowance; I have no money; I want you to take some home, and sell; "I did so; I had no intention of stealing it.
(Harradin received a good character.)
KNIGHT — GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Six Months.
HARRADIN — GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Eight Months.
[trialAccount t18440205-809]
809. DANIEL HENDERSON and JAMES TURNER were indicted for stealing 180lbs. weight of lead, value 1 l.; 3 s., the goods of Augustns Raymond, in a certain wharf, adjacent to the Thames.
MR. CUR WOOD conducted the Prosecution.
HAMOND JOBN GRIFFIN ( Thames police-constable, No. 33.) On the 13th of Jan. a man came and spoke to me, and in consequence of what he said I went into the shop of Bourne, a marine-store dealer, and found the prisoners with some lead—v told Henderson I wanted him—he said, "What for?"—I said, "The pig of lead"—they were standing before the counter, apparently in the act of rolling it—the dealer was in the act of looking at it—I called my brother officer in, and gave them into custody, and I carried the lead—they said they picked the lead up on Church-stairs shore.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Who gave you the information? A. I do not know—it was a man in Wapping Dock—he did not tell me where the men got the lead—I was going by, and he spoke to me.
GEOROE BOURNE , marine-store dealer, Green Bank, Wapping. Between ten and eleven o'clock at night I was called down, and told somebody wanted to sell some lead—my shop was shut—when I got down I saw two persons in the shop—I cannot say whether they were the prisoners—Idirectly afterwards saw two Thames police-officers—the two persons were taken—there was some lead in a scale on my counter—I cannot say who put it in—my wife let the men into the shop, and called me down—I cannot swear whether it was such a pig of lead as this that was in the scale—I would not swear to it.
Cross-examined. Q. Was your wife in the shop? A. No, she came down when the knock came, and she directly called me—I had no sooner got down than two police-officers came into the shop—I expect my wife did not put this lead into the scale—I saw nobody put it into the scale—this was between ten and eleven o'clock on Saturday night.
JOHN ATWELLS . In Jan. I was in the employ of Mr. Mead Terry Raymond; his brother's name is Augustus. My master's is the Thames Tunnel Wharf, Rotherhithe—I have the charge of the property on that wharf—on the night of the 13th of Jan. there were between 700 and 800 pigs of lead on the wharf, with one mark on them—I believe this pig produced to be one of them—I missed one on the Sunday morning which I know was there on the Saturday night—I found a small door open at the end of the shed, but I did not miss the pig till the policeman came—Henderson is a lighterman—I have known him some years—he knows our premises—Turner was employed in watermen's boats, and I have employed him myself for a short time—he knew our premises.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known Henderson? A. Four or five years—he was always a hard-working man, and honest, as far as I know.
AUGUSTUS RAYMOND . I live at Limetree-cottage, Rotherhithe—my brother is proprietor of the wharf—I am the owner of the lead—the wharf belongs to my brother—goods are landed there, and I have the carting of the property, and am answerable for it—some lead was landed from a barge in Jan., and I cart it as I think proper—-there were between 700 and 800 pigs of lead there, and they were all marked the same as this one is—on the Sunday morning the foreman gave me information, and I missed one pig.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see this lead? A. Yes, on the Saturday night, between six and seven o'clock—I cart the lead for my brother—he is the person who contracts with the owners of the lead—he pays me half-a-crown a ton for the cartage—this lead was lying on his premises—I have nothing to do with the owners—I should pay my brother if anything was gone.
MR. CUR WOOD . Q. Your brother is the wharfinger, the lead was landed on the wharf, and you have the contract for the cartage? A. Yes—if it is lost, I am responsible to my brother—if property is stolen from the wharf, my brother is answerable—I have to pay my brother, and he has to pay the party—my servant has to watch the wharf, and if he loses it, I have to pay—my brother's name is Mead Terry Raymond—this lead came for Pontifex's—directly the barge comes alongside, I put a watchman in, and I am answerable for it.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you see the whole of this lead on the wharf? A. Yes—the original parcel was 900 pigs—I saw it landed—I did not see it put into the barge—we have had several pigs since, which were different to this—here is the name of Grassington on one side of this pig, and I think there is an F on the other, and the coat of arms—I do not think there is a great quantity of lead of this description.
EDWARD EVERIST ( Thames police-inspector.) I know both the prisoners.
On the night on which the lead is said to have been stolen, I saw Turner near the prosecutor's premises, from five to ten minutes before ten o'clock.
(Henderson received a good character.)
HENDERSON— GUILTY . Aged 18.
TURNER— GUILTY . Aged 19.
Recommended to mercy.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18440205-810]
810. JOHN HARSANT was indicted for stealing 1 spoon, value 8 s., the goods of James Rogers.
JAMES ROGERS . I live at Union-cottage, Camberwell. I occasionally employed the prisoner at my house—on the 1st of Feb. I missed three spoons—I went with the policeman to the prisoner's house, and asked him where my spoons were—he said nothing for a minute or two, and then he said he knew nothing of them—his mother, who was in the room, said if he knew where my spoons were he was to tell her—he then said he had taken them, and given them to Ashley—we took Ashley—he was at Union-hall, and then the prisoner said he did not say he gave them to Ashley.
Prisoner. He said if I told him where they were he would not prosecute me. Witness. I did not.
GEORGE MORGAN ( police-constable P 211.) I heard the prisoner's mother ask him if he knew any thing of the spoons to tell Mr. Rogers, and she said to the prisoner, "Go into the passage and tell him"—I took hold of the prisoner, and said he should not go out, and then he said he gave them to Ashley.
GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Six Months.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18440205-811]
811. SARAH FOUNTAIN was indicted for stealing 20 yards of printed cotton value 12 s. 6 d., the goods of Walter Lyall.
WALTER LYALL . I keep a shop in Beckwith's-row, Lambeth. On the 26th of Jan. the prisoner came and said her name was Rose, and the lived with Mr. Jones, the grocer—she ordered some goods, but spent no money—after she was gone I missed a piece of print—this is it.
ROBERT COLE . I am a pawnbroker. This print was pawned by a female on the evening of the 26th of Jan.—this is the duplicate I gave.
WILLIAM GOODEVE ( police-constable V 163.) I took the prisoner—this duplicate was given me by Biggers.
LOUISA BIGGERS . I searched the prisoner, and found this duplicate on her.
Prisoner's Defence. A young woman said if I would pledge this for her she would give me the ticket.
GUILTY .— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18440205-812]
812. THOMAS CUNNINGHAM was indicted for stealing 1 gown, value 9 s.; 4 frocks, 8 s.; 1 waistcoat, 4 s.; 1 shift, 6 d.; 1 pillow-case, 6 d.; and 1 handkerchief, 6 d.; the goods of Edward Mears.
SOPHIA MEARS . I am the wife of Edward Mears—we live in St. George's-row. On the 7th of Feb. I missed this gown and other things from my front room—I had left the window closed, and when I went into the room again, between six and seven o'clock, it was open, and there were marks of feet on the chair.
JAMES BAKER ( police-constable, P 98.) I stopped the prisoner with these things in East-street, Wal worth—Iasked what he had got—he said some linen which he was going to take to his mother's to wash—I said, "I suppose she is" a laundress?"—he said, "Yes, and I got these from No. 12, Milford-street"—I said, "There is no such place"—he said, "If you come with me I will show you"—I went to a house in Belgrave-place—I said, "Is this the place?"—he said, "Yes"—I rapped at the door—a woman came—I asked if she had sent these things—she said, "No," and I took the prisoner to the station.
Prisoner. I told the policeman I picked them up at a street door.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Cojfined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18440205-813]
813. GRIFF GRIFFITHS was indicted for stealing, 3 washhnd-stands, value 3 l.; the goods of Robert James Price, his master.
ROBERT JAMBS PRICE . I live in Margaret-street, Haggerston, and am a cabinet-maker. The prisoner was my journeyman—I paid hin 5 s. a week, with his board, lodging, and washing—I had three wash hand-stands in Oct, last—on the 27th of Oct., there was a knock at the door—I sent my sister-in-law down to answer it—she said it was a person named Henley who wanted Griffiths—the prisoner then went, and was gone three quarters of an hour—when he returned, I said, "Who was that?"—he said, "Mr. Henley who came to tell me where to soil the stands, ever the water"—oa the 28th the prisoner and I went to sell die stands over the water—when we got there he said, "This is the shop, it is a man that knowa me well, and has takes money of my father; if you like I will take them in and sell them"—he took them, and I said, "Don't sell them for less than three sovereigns, they are worth 3 l.; 10 s. "—he was there for two hours, and then came out—he said he had sold them for 3 l.; but I must wait till six o'clock tor the money—he got off, and went home to my residence, and told my brother to go to Mott and Griffin's (which I had told the prisoner to tell him)—I did not see the prisoner again till he was taken about five weeks ago—Mr. Reed showed me the receipt for 2 l., 10 s. for the washhand-stands—this is it—it is the prisoner's writing.
Prisoner. Q. Can you say I was no partner of yours? A. I can.
Prisoner. Here is a card ( producing one with " G. Griffith and Price " on it) which you desired me to get printed; here is my name first on the card. Hiness. I know nothing of this card—here is my card, with my name only on it—I neither ordered nor paid for the others.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not go with me to Mr. Breton's when we drew two bills on him, I put my name first? he asked you whether it was true that I was partner with you, and you said, "Yes;" there is Mr. Breton in Court now; you signed your name under mine? A. It is totally false—when the prisoner came to me he said he had not had a meal for two days, nor a lodging for two nights—I lodged and boarded him two days, and two nights, and on the third day he asked me to give him a job—we gave him a job.
CHARLES ALLEN . I went with Mr. Price and the prisoner to the shop in the Westminster-bridge-road—we then went to a coffee shop, and the prisoner said, "You must go at seven o'clock for your money, I sold them for 3 l.;"—I have worked for Mr. Price five months—I never heard of the prisoner being a partner—I have seen him paid his wages 5 s. a week—he worked for 5 s. a week, and I have seen him paid his wages.
JOHN REED . I am shopman to Mr. James Bedell. On the 28th of Oct., about five o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came with three washhand-stands—I went and spoke to Mr. Bedell about them—I bought them for 2 l.; 10 s.—I paid the prisoner, and have the receipt in his writing.
HENRY TURPIN ( police-constable G 119.) I took the prisoner—I told him it was for running away with the stands of Mr. Price—he said, "That is all right; it is three months ago."
Prisoner's Defence. I went to work for Mr. Price, and after some time my father was going to the Cape of Good Hope, and Price, not knowing the cabinet business, told me if I would take 5 s. a week, and board, lodging, and washing, I should share in the profits, as it was all my connexion; I said, "Very well;" if Mr. Stallybrass is here, he knows the bills were made out in my name; we drew two separate bills on a customer, and there is my name first on the bills; I paid them both to Mr. Stallybrass, and the bills were, "To Messrs. Griffiths, and L. and It. Price;" this was the only card I could get from a friend; the other cards were taken away, and the invoices and bills with my name on them.
ROBERT FICKLING . I am a cabinet-maker, and have lived upwards of two years at No. 36, Brown's-lane, Spitalfields. I was employed by the prisoner—I was not then aware it was a partnership, but I understood three or four days afterwards that it was a partnership—the prisoner employed me—I worked piece work—I was employed in making washhand-standa I believe—I am sure I was, and I made dressing-tables—the same person employed me to do them—no other person employed me—the prisoner was the active man for the partnership concern as I understood—I was paid sometimes by Price, and sometimes by the prisoner—Robert Price said, "You must not suppose this is all Mr. Griffith's concern; I am the principal partner."
COURT. Q. Are you sure he said these words, "I am the partner?" A. No, his elder brother, Mr. Lemuel Price is the elder partner—he said, "It is ours"—his elder brother and his—I always understood the prisoner was partner.
COURT to ROBERT PRICE. Q. Do you know this witness? A. Yes, the prisoner has robbed him as well as me—he went with me and the constable to search the prisoner's lodging for his tools—he has been in constant intercourse with the prisoner—I never told him that it was not all Griffith's concern—I told him it was not Griffith's concern, but mine and my brother's—my brother and I have always been together, but he had nothing to do with this concern, it was mine—I am sure the washhand-stands were not my
brother's, they were mine—he had nothing to do with them—I distinctly swear the prisoner was no partner of mine in any way—I paid the work-people, but sometimes if I was out the prisoner might do it—I gave him the money to pay the men—these cards with the prisoner's name I never saw—he had no share in any way—when he wanted clothes I ordered them, and paid for them, and put them down to his account—the washhand-stands were made in Oct.—I was married in June—before I was married my brother and I divided the cabinet work, but after I was married he gave it up to me—I attended to it, and he had none of the profits of it.
Prisoner. I took on both Fickling and Hanley; previous to my leaving I had been very ill, and subject to fits, and they said that they had got a lawyer to draw up a paper, and I was to sign it that I had no claim on the concern; Mr. Henley told me of it.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Nine Months.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18440205-814]
814. PHILIP BOWDEN was indicted for stealing 1 pewter pot, value 2 s., the goods of James Gale; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM PEARSON . I am servant to Mr. James Gale, who keeps the Hen and Chickens at Walworth-common. This quart pot is his—I was going round for my pots last Friday morning, between nine and ten o'clock—I saw the prisoner pass, facing me, and he chucked a bag on his back—I ran and caught him, and asked what he had got in the bag—he said, "Nothing"—I took the bag off his back, and shook this pot out, and asked where he got it—he said a boy gave it him to carry—it had been at a customer's honse—I had seen it safe at my master's on Thursday night.
JOHN SMITH ( police-constable P 60.) I took the prisoner, and have the pot.
Prisoner's Defence. A person asked me to carry it.
JOHN GIFFORD . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from the clerk of the peace of Surrey—( read)—I was present at the trial—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Six Months, and Twice Whipped.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, THE 4 TH OF MARCH.
